# To the READER.

their errors, it led me into a serious consideration of their cause; and I found that it was impossible for those deluded searchers to waste away in such absurd operations <note>original: "Operations"; the practical chemical experiments of alchemy.</note> if they did not lack a sufficient theory of this art.

Then meditating further, and finding that the said theory (which is the main pillar and foundation to build upon) is not to be acquired except by the constant reading of good books, I at last began to consider those authors who are available in the English language <note>original: "English Tongue"</note>. I found that there are really too few of them, and that it was chiefly to be attributed to this lack <note>original: "Want"; a deficiency or absence of something.</note> that people labor so much in vain, and so contrary to nature.

# To the READER.

It is true that some people, looking only at the sheer number of books, will think there are enough authors—perhaps even too many existing—who have written about the **Hermetic Art** <note>original: "Hermetick Art"; the philosophical and practical system of alchemy and spiritual transformation attributed to Hermes Trismegistus.</note> in the *English* language. However, others who have gained some knowledge in this **Divine Science**, and who know how to distinguish good authors from **sophists** <note>original: "Sophisters"; in this context, "puffers" or fraudulent alchemists who use deceptive language to hide their lack of real knowledge.</note>, will easily admit that there is a real lack <note>original: "Want"</note> of them. 

For there are only a few who wrote in the *English* language who may be considered masters, or at least by whom a seeker of this mysterious art might be instructed. As for those deceptive authors who have increased the number of such books and treated an art which they did not know, nor understand even in part, the more numerous they are, 

<sig>A 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: the</meta>

<summary>The author argues that despite a high volume of English alchemical texts, most are written by unqualified "sophists," leaving a shortage of reliable guides for genuine students of the Hermetic Art.</summary>

<page-num>viii</page-num>
# To the READER.

...the more harmful they prove to a beginner who happens to read them. It is certain that they may easily lead him off the right path, but they can never lead him toward the true way.

As for those books that have been translated from other languages, they are of little use when they have been translated to serve some private agenda rather than to be instructive to those who study this science. This is especially true when they have been translated by people who had no knowledge of the terms and operations of <term>chemistry</term> <note>original: "Chymistry"</note>. I have no doubt it will be affirmed by all those who (understanding the German language) have read *Basil Valentine* <note>Basil Valentine was the masked identity of a 15th-century alchemist, supposedly a Benedictine monk, whose influential writings on antimony and alchemy were widely translated in the 17th century.</note> in his own native tongue, that the translation of that volume which con-

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of the translation of Basil Valentine's works and introduces the author's motivation for providing new, accurate translations of alchemical treatises to steer students away from "unnatural operations" (failed or dangerous chemical experiments).</meta>

<page-num>ix</page-num>

# To the Reader.

...tains his *Last Will and Testament* <note>original: "Will and Testament, &c."</note>, differs widely from the original; indeed, I believe I could easily convince devotees of this art that this work is a poor translation of a bad edition.

Regarding other books, I will say nothing against them, as it is not my intention to seek out the faults of other translators. However, considering the reasons mentioned above, I realized that having the works of reputable authors translated would be the most effective way to provide those who love and study this science with a solid theoretical foundation. This, in turn, might encourage them to stop their misguided experiments <note>original: "unnatural Operations"</note>. Therefore, I decided to undertake this work myself and chose the following treatises to be the first ones to appear in an *English translation* <note>original: "English Dress"</note>.

# To the Reader.

The first of these treatises was written by a German philosopher under the title *The Ancient War of the Knights*. It was initially accepted as the work of a reputable author and true master by everyone who had gained any knowledge of this science <note>original: "Science"; here referring to alchemy</note>. Furthermore, this treatise later received authoritative validation from a second work: a commentary written in *French* by another learned philosopher. Following his instructive and scholarly commentary, this second author added "six Keys." Because of their clarity and brilliant explanations, these keys are second to none and are of the greatest possible use to any student of this art <note>original: "Art"; referring to the practice of alchemy</note>.

Since the combined work is so excellent, there is no doubt that

# To the READER.

those who apply themselves to gaining knowledge by reading good authors will find infinite benefit from perusing it. Since the aforementioned treatise, called **THE ANCIENT WAR OF THE KNIGHTS**, was written in the *German language*, I have gone to the effort and expense of obtaining an original copy of it. My goal was that the lovers of this science <note>original: "Science"; referring to alchemy</note> might also have a translation of the same, allowing them to draw water from the spring itself <note>meaning to study the original source rather than a secondary translation</note>. Additionally, by comparing this version with the first <note>the French translation mentioned on the previous page</note>, they might see and correct those passages that have suffered from being translated a second time <note>the author is concerned about "translation loss" occurring when a text is translated from German to French, and then French to English</note>.

I hope that no one will object that the style of this work is not in keeping with modern refinement <note>original: "Politeness"; referring to the polished, elegant literary style expected of formal writing in the 1700s</note>, if they are kind enough to consider that it is not intended to be a textbook on grammar,

Therefore, it is better to stick to the author's meaning in a less pleasing style than to deviate from it by using more polished expressions.

It is also for the same reason that whenever the *German* source has a word or sentence that might carry a double meaning or interpretation, both of them have been provided. <header>To the Reader.</header>

*in the Fire*—The original says indeed *in the Fire*, but because the Germans, when speaking of chemical operations and mentioning *Fire*, often mean *Digestion* <note>original: "Digestion"; in alchemy, this refers to the process of exposing a substance to gentle, continuous heat over a long period, mimicking the warmth of a living body</note>, which is performed by *Fire*; therefore, the phrase [*or in Digestion*] has been added, so that the reader might have both meanings and choose which of the two he judges most proper.

    I could easily have added my opinion on several passages in this work; for instance, on page 41, where mention is made of the stars of *Venus and Diana* <note>In alchemical symbolism, "Diana" refers to the Moon</note>. Those two signs or characters, when put together, produce the one that signifies *Mercury*; for if the character of horned *Diana* [☽] is placed upon that of *Venus* [♀], it yields a character in this form ☿. But I omit such commentary, lest I should be thought

### THE
# PREFACE
### Of the Author of the
## COMMENTARY,
### Translated from the French.

### NOTICE <note>original: "ADVERTISEMENT"</note>

One is quite convinced that there are already too many books that discuss Hermetic philosophy <term>Hermetic philosophy: An ancient spiritual and alchemical tradition based on the presumed teachings of Hermes Trismegistus, focusing on the pursuit of the "Great Work" or the Philosopher's Stone.</term>; and that unless one were to write about this science plainly, without ambiguity <note>original: "Equivocation"</note> and without allegory (which none of the

<meta>catchword: Sages</meta>

<summary>This page begins the author's preface, asserting that while many books exist on Hermetic philosophy, they are often obscured by allegory and ambiguous language.</summary>
<keywords>Hermetic philosophy, alchemy, preface, allegory, plain speech, Sages</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's preface from page xv, concluding a sentence about the rarity of plain speech among alchemical "Sages" and explaining the rationale for providing a commentary on an existing text rather than writing a new one.</meta>

<page-num>xvi</page-num>
<header>The PREFACE.</header>

(as the sages will always do), it would be better to remain silent than to fill the world with new works. These works serve more to clutter the spirit [or minds] of those who apply themselves to penetrate the philosophical mysteries than to set them on the true path that leads to the desired end they seek. 

For this reason, it has been decided that interpreting a reliable author who treats this sublime philosophy with sound reasoning would be more useful to the "children of the science" <term>children of the science: A common term for students or initiates of alchemy and Hermeticism.</term> than some new work full of parables, adorned with the ingenious expressions that adepts <term>adepts: Individuals who have supposedly mastered the "Great Work" of alchemy.</term> know how to craft when they discuss this great art. Or rather, when they write only to inform those who already possess the mastery <term>magistery: (Latin: magisterium) Also known as the "Great Work," referring to the ultimate achievement in alchemy, often the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</term>—or those seeking it—that they have had the good fortune to achieve it. 

Indeed, most of the philosophers <term>philosophers: In this context, the author refers to "chemical philosophers" or alchemists.</term> who have written about it have done so more to speak of the success with which God has blessed their work than to provide the necessary instruction to those who devote themselves...

<meta>catchword: selves</meta>

<summary>The author argues that providing a clear commentary on a reliable alchemical text is more beneficial to students than publishing new, cryptic works that often focus more on the author's success than on practical instruction.</summary>
<keywords>Hermetic philosophy, alchemy, commentary, adepts, magistery, philosophical mysteries, children of the science, alchemists</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's explanation of why most alchemical writers are intentionally obscure and introduces the specific text being commented upon: *The Ancient War of the Knights*.</meta>

<page-num>xvii</page-num>
<header>The PREFACE.</header>
<sig>(a)</sig>

selves to the study of this sacred science. This is so true that most of them do not even hesitate to sincerely admit that this has been their chief goal when they composed their books on this subject.

The short treatise titled ***The Ancient War of the Knights*** <note>original: "The ANTIENT WAR OF THE KNIGHTS"; this refers to a famous alchemical text originally published in German as *Uralter Ritter-Krieg*.</note> has, without contradiction, deserved the approval of all the sages [or *wise men*] and of those who have any knowledge of ***Hermetic Philosophy*** <term>Hermetic Philosophy: A tradition of spiritual and physical alchemy based on the supposed writings of Hermes Trismegistus.</term>. It is written in the form of a dialogue, in a very plain and natural style that displays the marks of truth in every way. Yet, despite its simplicity, it does not lack depth; it is solid in its reasoning and convincing in its proofs. In fact, there is not a single word that does not carry its own weight, and upon which a lengthy commentary could not be written.

This work was composed in the ***German language*** by a true ***philosopher*** <term>philosopher: In this context, the author refers to a "chemical philosopher" or an adept in the art of alchemy.</term>, whose name is unknown.

<summary>The author introduces the source material for this volume—a German alchemical dialogue titled *The Ancient War of the Knights*—praising its clarity, logical rigor, and status among experts in Hermetic philosophy.</summary>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the anonymous author of *The Ancient War of the Knights* and traces the publication history and translation errors of the work.</meta>

<page-num>xviii</page-num>
<header>The PREFACE.</header>

[The author is] unknown. It appeared in print at Leipzig in the year 1604. Faber of Montpellier translated it into Latin, and it was from this Latin edition that the French translation was produced. This version was printed in Paris by d'Houry and placed at the end of a book containing the *French Turba* <note>The *Turba Philosophorum*, or *Assembly of the Philosophers*, is one of the oldest and most famous Latin alchemical texts.</note>, *The Word Left Behind* <note>original Latin: *verbum dimissum*</note>, and the works of Drebellius <note>Cornelis Drebbel (1572–1633), a Dutch engineer and alchemist.</note>, which together make up one volume. 

However, whether Faber did not well understand the German language or instead intentionally falsified the original, there are corrupted passages in these two translations. These are so clearly false that they have caused many to despise <note>original: "contemned"</note> this little work, even though it otherwise seems to be highly esteemed. 

Because truth and falsehood cannot exist together in the same subject, and because it was easy to judge that these translations were not done faithfully, a philosopher of extraordinary knowledge and merit did,

<meta>catchword: for</meta>

<summary>The author traces the publication history of the treatise from its 1604 Leipzig edition through flawed Latin and French translations, noting that these errors caused some readers to unfairly dismiss the work.</summary>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of a dedicated scholar who spent ten years searching for the original German manuscript of "The Ancient War of the Knights" to correct the corruptions found in earlier Latin and French versions.</meta>

<page-num>xix</page-num>
<header>The PREFACE.</header>
<sig>(a 2)</sig>

...to satisfy his curiosity on this point, he took the trouble of searching for more than ten years to find the original German version of this little treatise. Having finally found it, he had it accurately translated into Latin. *This new translation is taken from that copy and performed with the greatest possible accuracy.* The quality of the original can be seen here by the truth that clearly appears in various places which have been restored to their former meaning—sections that were not only altered, but completely changed. One may judge this by the passage marked thirty-four, where the first translation says, following the Latin of Faber: "No one can attain our Mercury <note>In alchemy, "Mercury" refers to a primary philosophical principle or a "living spirit" within metals, rather than just the common liquid metal.</note> except from eight soft bodies, and none can be prepared without the other" <note>original Latin: *Mercurium nostrum nemo assequi potest; nisi ex mollibus octo corporibus neque ullum absque altero parari potest.*</note>. No one can attain our Mercury in any other way than from the eight soft bodies <term>The author points this out as a ridiculous error; in alchemy there are usually seven metals/bodies, and "eight" would have signaled to a practitioner that the text was a forgery or nonsense.</term>, nor can one be prepared without the other. *This treatise needed nothing else to make it despised.* <note>The author means that this single error was so significant that it was enough on its own to cause readers to dismiss the entire book as worthless.</note>

<summary>A scholar spent a decade locating the original German text to produce a faithful translation, correcting significant errors found in earlier versions—such as a nonsensical claim about "eight soft bodies"—that had caused the work to be unfairly discredited.</summary>

Since these types of *writings* are not suitable for those who have not yet gained even a spark of the secret *philosophy* <note>original: "secret Philosophy"; referring to the esoteric study of alchemy</note>, the most insightful readers will easily see that it was thought better to skip several things—which perhaps might have deserved an *interpretation*—than to explain generally everything that might still cause difficulty for young beginners in this great *art* <note>original: "Art"; the "Great Art" or *Ars Magna* of alchemy</note>.

Since the first of these *dialogues* recounts the *victory of the stone*, and the other explains the reasons and shows the foundation of its triumph, it seemed that this book could not be published under a more appropriate title than *The Hermetic Triumph: or, The Victorious Philosophical Stone* <note>original: "Philosophical Stone"; usually referred to today as the Philosopher's Stone</note>. # THE
## *General Explanation*
### OF THE
# EMBLEM,
## Translated from the *French*.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'I' set within a square frame featuring ornate floral scrollwork and a small stylized face at the top center.</image-desc>

IT should not be expected to find a detailed explanation here—one that would pull back the curtains spread over the <term>philosophical enigma</term> <note>original: "philosophick Enigma"; a common term in alchemy for a riddle or puzzle that conceals a secret process</note> to show the Truth completely naked. If that were done, nothing would remain but to burn all the writings of the philosophers. The wise would no longer have any advantage over the ignorant; both would be equally skilled in this <term>wonderful art</term> <note>original: "wonderful Art"; a reference to alchemy, often called "the Art" or "the Great Work"</note>.

IT should therefore be considered sufficient to see in this figure, as in a <note>original: "Looking-Glass"</note> mirror, a summary <note>original: "Abridgement"</note> of the entire secret philosophy contained in this little book, in which all the parts of this emblem are explained as clearly as is permitted.

<summary>This page introduces the general explanation of the book's central emblem, noting that while it summarizes the secret philosophy, it will not reveal everything plainly so as to preserve the mystery of the "wonderful art."</summary>
<keywords>Emblem, philosophical enigma, alchemy, secret philosophy, explanation, wonderful art</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the "General Explication" of the alchemical emblem, describing the symbolic process of the "Work" and the transformation of materials into the final philosopher's stone.</meta>

THOSE who are initiated into the philosophical mysteries will easily and immediately understand the meaning hidden beneath this figure. But those who do not possess this enlightenment must consider here, in general terms, a mutual correspondence between the heavens and the earth, mediated by the sun and moon, which act like the secret bonds of this philosophical union.

THEY will see in the practice of the Work <note>original: "Worck"; likely referring to the *Magnum Opus* or Great Work of alchemy</note>, two symbolic streams which, by secretly mixing together, give birth to the mysterious triangular stone that is the foundation of the Art.

THEY will see a secret and natural fire, whose spirit penetrates the stone and turns it into vapors <note>original: "sublimes it in Vapours"</note> that condense within the vessel.

THEY will see what power the refined <note>original: "sublimed"</note> stone receives from the sun and moon—its "father" and "mother"—from whom it immediately inherits its first crown of perfection.

THEY will see in the continuation of the practice (or the progress of the work) that the Art grants this divine liquor a double crown of perfection through the conversion of elements and the extraction and purification <note>original: "Depuration"</note> of the principles. Through this, it becomes the mysterious "Rod of *Mercury*", which performs such surprising transformations <note>original: "Metamorphosings"</note>.

THEY will see that this same Mercury, like a *Phoenix* reborn in the fire, reaches the final perfection of the "fixed sulfur of the philosophers" through the <term>magistery</term> <note>original: "Magistery"; the technical process or mastery required to complete the alchemical transformation</note>. This gives it supreme power over the three realms <note>original: "Genders [or Reigns]"; referring to the animal, vegetable, and mineral kingdoms</note> of nature, of which the triple crown (surmounted by the symbolic figure of the world) is the most essential characteristic.

<summary>This page describes the symbolic stages of the alchemical process, from the initial "triangular stone" to the final "fixed sulfur," emphasizing the roles of the sun, moon, and mercury.</summary>

I protest to you before God, and upon the (eternal) salvation of my soul, with a sincere heart touched with compassion for those who have been for a long time in this great search; and (I give you notice,) all you who esteem this wonderful art, that <margin>1.</margin> our whole work takes its birth <note>original: "Nativity"</note> ($\ast$) from one thing alone, and that in this thing the work finds its perfection, without needing any other thing whatsoever, except to <margin>2.</margin> be dissolved ($\ast$) and solidified <note>original: "coagulated"; to change from a liquid to a solid or semi-solid state.</note>, which it must do of itself, without the assistance of any foreign thing.

When we put ice into a vessel placed on the fire, we see that the heat makes it <margin>3.</margin> dissolve into water; ($\ast$) we must use the same way with our stone, which only requires the help of the artist, the operation of his hands, and the action of <margin>4.</margin> natural fire. For it will never be dissolved of itself, even if it should remain forever on the earth; it is for that reason we must assist it—in such a manner, however, that we add nothing to it which is foreign or contrary to it.

## The RECITAL <note>original: "Recital" – here meaning a formal narration or account of a dispute.</note>

Gold and *Mercury* went one day, weapons in hand, to give battle to and subdue the *Stone*. *Gold*, fueled by fury, began to speak like this:

### GOLD.

"How do you have the audacity to elevate yourself above me and my brother *Mercury*, and to claim a higher status than us? You, 
<margin>8.</margin>
who are nothing but a (*) worm swollen with poison! <note>Alchemists often referred to unrefined or toxic minerals as "worms" or "dragons" before they were purified into medicine.</note> Do you not know that I am the most precious, the most durable, and the leader of all the metals? Do you not know that monarchs, princes, and nations all believe their riches consist of me and my brother *Mercury*? You, on the other hand, are the dangerous enemy of both men and metals. For this reason, the most skilled physicians never stop proclaiming and praising the remarkable virtues <note>original: "Vertues" – in this context, it refers to the inherent power or medicinal efficacy of a substance.</note> I possess 
<margin>9.</margin>
(*) to restore and preserve the health of the entire world."

### The STONE.

To these words, which were full of anger, the Stone answered calmly: "My dear **Gold**, why are you not instead..."

## GOLD

It is God himself who has given me the honor, the reputation, and the glittering brightness which makes me so esteemed; it is for that reason that I am sought after by everyone. One of my greatest perfections is to be a metal unchangeable in the fire and out of the fire. Therefore, all the world loves me and runs after me. But you—you are only a fugitive <note>original: "Fugitive"; in alchemy, this refers to a "volatile" substance that evaporates or escapes when heated</note> and a <margin>10.</margin> cheat that abuses all men. This is seen in the fact that you fly away and escape out of the hands of those who work with you.

## The STONE

It is true, my dear Gold, it is God who has given you the honor, the durability, and the beauty which makes you precious. It is for that reason that you are obliged to return eternal thanks to the divine bounty, and not to despise others as you do. For I can tell you that you are not that *Gold* of which the writings of the *Philosophers* <note>The "Philosophers" refers to the ancient and medieval alchemists who sought the secret of transformation.</note> make mention; <margin>11.</margin> but that *Gold* is hidden in my bosom. <header>The HERMETICAL</header>

It is true, I admit it: I flow in the fire (and I do not stay there). Nevertheless, you know very well that God and Nature have given me this quality, and that it must be so. This is because my fluidity <note>original: "Fluidity"; in this context, it refers to the substance's volatility—its tendency to evaporate or melt when heated.</note> turns to the advantage of the Artist <note>In alchemical literature, the "Artist" is the alchemist or practitioner of the Great Work.</note> who knows <margin>12. (*)</margin> how to extract it. Know, nonetheless, that my soul remains constant within me, and that she is more stable and more fixed than you are—even as much *Gold* as you are—and more so than all your brothers and companions. Neither water nor fire, whatever they may be, can destroy or consume her, even if they were to act upon her for as long as the world shall last.

It is not my fault, then, if I am sought after by artists who do not know how they should work with me, nor in what way I should be prepared. They often mix me with foreign things that are entirely contrary to my nature. They add water, powders, and other such things to me, which destroy my nature and the properties that are essential to me. Because of this, <margin>13. (*)</margin> hardly one person in a hundred is found who truly works with me. They all apply themselves to searching for the truth of the Art <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy, the "Hermetic Art" of transforming base metals into noble ones.</note> within you and your brother *Mercury*;

<summary>The Stone argues that while it appears volatile in fire, its inner "soul" is more fixed and durable than gold. it blames the failures of alchemists on their poor methods and their tendency to mix it with improper substances.</summary>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue where the Stone (the "First Matter") is rebuking Gold for deceiving alchemists who try to use refined metals as the starting point of the Great Work.</meta>

<header>-> TRIUMPH. <-</header>

It is for that reason that they all err, and it is in this that their works are false. They themselves are a good example of this; for it is fruitlessly that they employ their *gold*, and endeavor to destroy it. Nothing remains for them from all of that but extreme poverty, to which they see themselves at last reduced.

It is you, *gold*, who are the first cause of this ill fortune. You know very well that without me it is impossible to make any *gold* or any *silver* which shall be perfect, and that I alone have this wonderful advantage. Why, therefore, do you permit almost the whole world to lay the foundation of their operations upon you and upon *mercury*? If you still had any remnant of honesty, you would prevent men from abandoning themselves to a most certain loss; but as you do quite the contrary instead, I may truly maintain that it is you alone who are a cheat.

### G O L D.

I will convince you by the authority of the *philosophers* <note>original: "Philosophers"; in alchemical texts, this refers to the great masters of the Hermetic Art, such as Geber, Lully, or Hermes Trismegistus.</note> that the truth of the Art may be accomplished with me. # The HERMETICAL

They declare themselves plainly about this when they tell you that they reveal the least of the secret of their Art when they speak most openly and in the common way of speaking. On the <margin>17.</margin> contrary, they affirm that when they use metaphors, figures, and parables, it is actually in those parts of their writings that they disclose their Art. For after the *philosophers* <note>In alchemical texts, "philosophers" refers to the "natural philosophers" or adepts who have successfully achieved the Great Work.</note> have discussed *gold* and *mercury*, they never fail to declare and assure us afterward that their *gold* is not common gold (or *Sol* <note>original: "Sol"; the Latin word for Sun, often used in alchemy to represent common gold.</note>) and that their *mercury* is not common mercury. Here is the reason why:

Gold is a perfect metal. Because of the perfection nature has already given it, it cannot be advanced any further by Art to a more perfect degree. Therefore, in whatever way one works with *gold*, and whatever techniques one uses—even if one were to extract its color and its tincture <note>A "tincture" in alchemy is a spiritual or medicinal substance capable of infusing its properties (like color or "perfection") into another substance.</note> a hundred times—the artist will never produce more *gold*. Nor will they ever transform <note>original: "tinge"</note> a greater quantity of metal than the amount of color and tincture that was originally in the *gold* from which it was extracted. For this reason, the <margin>18.</margin> *philosophers* say that we ought to seek perfection in imperfect things, and that we shall find

Many alchemical works were attributed to him after his death, making him a central figure in "pseudo-Lullian" alchemy.</note>, whom you have cited to me, is of the same sentiment. He assures us that whatever is to be improved must not already be perfect; because in that which is perfect, there is nothing left to change, and one would sooner destroy its nature than add anything to its perfection.

### GOLD.

I am not ignorant that the *philosophers* speak in this manner; yet this may be applied to my brother *Mercury*, who is as yet imperfect. But if someone joins both of us together, he then receives from me the perfection which he lacks. For he is of the *feminine sex*, and I am of the *masculine sex*; this is why the *philosophers* say that the Art is one entirely homogeneous <note>original: "homogeneal"; meaning a substance that is uniform in its structure and nature throughout.</note> thing. You see an example of this in the reproduction of human beings, for no child can be born without the union of male and female—that is to say, without the conjunction of the one with the other. We have the like example in animals and in all living beings.

### The STONE.

<margin>19.</margin>It is true that your brother *Mercury* is imperfect, and consequently, he is not the "*Mercury* of the wise" <note>The "Mercury of the wise" (mercurius philosophorum) refers to a theoretical, purified substance sought by alchemists, distinct from common liquid quicksilver.</note>. Therefore, even if you were joined together and someone kept you in the fire for many years to try to unite you perfectly, the same thing would always happen: as soon as the *Mercury* feels the action of the fire, it separates from you; it sublimes <note>original: "sublimed"; the process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, often used for purification.</note>, flies away, and leaves you alone at the bottom. Even if someone dissolves you in *aqua fortis* <note>original: "Aqua-fortis"; Latin for "strong water," referring to nitric acid, a powerful solvent used to separate gold from other metals.</note>, reduces you into a single mass, melts you, distills you, or coagulates you, you will never produce anything but a powder and a red precipitate. If someone makes a projection <note>original: "Projection"; the final stage of the alchemical process, where the Philosopher's Stone is cast upon a base metal to transmute it into gold.</note> of this powder onto an imperfect metal, it does not tinge <note>original: "tinges"; in alchemy, to "tinge" is to permanently change the color and internal nature of a metal.</note> it; instead, one finds only as much *gold* as was put in at the beginning, and your brother *Mercury* leaves you and flies away.

Behold, these are the experiments that those who apply themselves to the search for *chemistry* <note>original: "Chymistry"; here referring to practical alchemy or proto-chemistry.</note> have performed to their great loss <note>original: "Damage"; meaning financial ruin or wasted effort.</note> over many years. See also the point where all the knowledge they have acquired through their labors ends.

## GOLD.

What you have said is not sufficient proof; for though the *Philosophers* <note>original: "Philosophers"; in alchemical literature, this refers to the ancient and medieval authorities on the Great Work, such as Hermes Trismegistus or Geber.</note> speak of only one thing, which encloses within itself the four elements: and which [thing] has a body, a soul, and a spirit; and that by this "thing" they would have us understand the (*Physical*) Tincture <note>The "Physical Tincture" is the finished medicinal substance or "Elixir" capable of transforming base metals into gold.</note>; at such time when the same has been brought to its highest (*perfection*), which is the point they aim for; yet this thing must, at its beginning, be composed of me, who am *Gold*, and of my brother, who is *Mercury*, as being (both together) the *Male Seed* and the *Female Seed*, as has been said before. *Philosophers* have sought knowledge at its source for many years without ceasing. I have read the *Emerald Table* <note>original: "smaragdine Table," a short, cryptic text foundational to Hermeticism</note>, the *Seven Chapters of Hermes* with their commentaries, and the works of Geber <note>An influential 8th-century Arabic alchemist</note>, the *Assembly of the Philosophers* <note>original: "the Turba" — *Turba Philosophorum*, one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts</note>, the *Rosary of the Philosophers* <note>original: "Rosary" — *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century treatise</note>, the *Theater of Chemistry* <note>original: "Theatrum" — likely the *Theatrum Chemicum*, a massive compendium of alchemical writings</note>, the *Library of Chemical Philosophers* <note>original: "Bibliotheque" — likely the *Bibliothèque des Philosophes Chimiques*</note>, the *Chemical Cabinet*, and particularly Artephius, Arnaldus de Villa Nova, Ramon Llull, Bernard of Trevisan, Nicolas Flamel, Denis Zachaire, and many others, both ancient and modern, whom I will not name. Among others, I have read Basil Valentine, the Cosmopolite <note>original: "Cosmopolita," a pseudonym often used by Michael Sendivogius</note>, and Philalethes <note>A pseudonym for the 17th-century alchemist Eirenaeus Philalethes</note>.

I assure you I struggled immensely to find the essential point upon which they all should agree. They use such different expressions, which often seem to be opposite. Some speak of the "Matter" <term>The primary substance used in the alchemical Great Work</term> in abstract terms, while others use complex ones. Some describe only certain qualities of the Matter, while others focus on completely different properties. Some consider it in its purely natural state, while others speak of it in terms of the perfections it receives from the Art <note>The "Art" refers to the practice of alchemy itself</note>. All of this together throws a person into such a labyrinth of difficulties that it is no wonder most of those who read the *philosophers* reach entirely different conclusions.

I was not satisfied merely to read the primary authors you directed me to...

once over; I read them over again and again, as often as I thought I could draw new instructions from them, either concerning the true Matter <term>The primary substance used in the alchemical Great Work</term>, or its various preparations, upon which the entire success of the Work <term>The Great Work (Magnum Opus), the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone</term> depends. ->EUDOXUS.<-

I am ready to satisfy your requests, and I shall be very glad to hear you address the essential point. I am resolved to conceal nothing from you that might serve for the instruction you feel you need. However, I believe it would be appropriate for me to first make some remarks that will contribute greatly to clearing up some important passages of the tract you mentioned.

Note then, that the term *Stone* is used in many different ways, and particularly in regard to the three different stages <note>original: "Stations"</note> of the Work. This is why *Geber* <note>Geber (Jabir ibn Hayyan) was a legendary 8th-century Arab polymath and alchemist whose writings laid the foundation for European alchemy.</note> says that there are three Stones, which are the three medicines corresponding to the three degrees of perfection in the Work. 

*   The Stone of the first order is the Matter of the *Philosophers* <term>The "Prima Materia" or raw starting material of the Great Work.</term> perfectly purified and reduced into a pure mercurial substance. 
*   The Stone of the second order is the same Matter decocted <term>Heated or concentrated to extract its essence.</term>, digested, and fixed <term>Made solid or non-volatile so it can withstand heat.</term> into an incombustible Sulphur. 
*   The Stone of the third order is the very same Matter fermented, multiplied <term>A process to increase the quantity and transformative power of the Stone.</term>, and pushed to the final perfection of a fixed, permanent Tincture <term>The final medicinal or transformative substance, capable of tingeing/coloring other metals.</term>,

<meta>catchword: tingeing ;</meta>

<summary>Eudoxus explains that the term "Stone" refers to three different stages of alchemical development: a purified mercurial substance, an incombustible sulfur, and a final permanent tincture.</summary>
<keywords>Eudoxus, Geber, three stones, mercurial substance, incombustible sulphur, tincture, alchemical stages</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of the three "Stones" or medicines in the alchemical process, specifically distinguishing between the raw material (the Stone of the Philosophers) and the finished product (the Philosophic Stone).</meta>

<page-num>33</page-num>
<header>TRIUMPH.</header>
<sig>D</sig>

...tingeing <note>original: "tinging"; the ability of the Stone to color or transform the nature of a metal.</note>. And these three Stones are the three medicines for the three kinds of work.

Note further that there is a great difference between the *Stone of the Philosophers* and the *Philosophic Stone*. The first is the "Subject of Philosophy" <note>In this context, "Philosophy" refers to Alchemy, and its "subject" is the *Prima Materia* or raw starting material.</note>, considered in its first state of preparation. In this state, it is truly a stone because it is solid, hard, heavy, brittle, and easily broken. It is a "body" (says *Philalethes* <note>Eirenaeus Philalethes, a 17th-century alchemist whose identity is often associated with George Starkey.</note>) because it flows in fire like a metal; and yet it is a "spirit," for it is completely volatile <term>Capable of evaporating or being reduced to gas when heated.</term>. 

"It is the compound and the stone that contains the humidity that flows in the fire," says *Arnoldus* <note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemical writer.</note> in his letter to the King of Naples. It is in this state that it is a substance midway between a metal and mercury (as the Abbot *Synesius* <note>An early alchemical author, often identified with the 4th-century Bishop Synesius of Cyrene.</note> expresses it). Finally, it is in this state that *Geber* <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan, the foundational 8th-century Arab alchemist.</note> considers it when he says in two places in his *Summa* <note>The *Summa Perfectionis Magisterii* (The Sum of Perfection), one of the most influential alchemical texts of the Middle Ages.</note>: "Take our stone—that is to say," he says, "the matter of our stone." It is as if he had said, "Take the *Philosopher's Stone*, which is the raw material for the *Philosophic Stone*."

The *Philosophic Stone* is, therefore, the same *Stone of the Philosophers*, but only after it has been raised to the perfection of the "third order" by the secret Magistery <term>The "Mastery" or the specialized technical process of the Great Work.</term>. In this final state, it transmutes all imperfect metals into pure gold or silver, depending on the nature of the ferment <term>An additive (usually gold or silver) used to "ripen" the Stone and determine its final power.</term>...

<margin>1.</margin>
You need not doubt that this treatise was written by the hand of a true Adept <term>A master of alchemy who has successfully achieved the "Great Work" or created the Philosopher's Stone.</term>, and consequently it deserves the respect and approval of *philosophers* <note>In alchemical texts, "philosophers" almost always refers to fellow alchemists.</note>. made from only one *thing*, of only one and the same *kind* <note>original: "Species"</note>.

->PYROPHILUS.<-

That is the very first of the points that raised some doubts <note>original: "Scruples"</note> in my mind; for it seems to me one may reasonably doubt that perfection ought to be sought in only one and the same substance, and that without adding anything to it, one could be able to make all things from it. The *philosophers*, on the contrary, say that we must not only remove the superfluous parts <note>original: "Superfluities"</note> from the matter, but that what is lacking must also be added to it.

->EUDOXUS.<-

It is easy to free you from that doubt by using this comparison: in the same way that juices extracted from various herbs—once purified from their dregs <term>original: "Fæces"; in alchemy, this refers to the solid residue, sediment, or impurities left over after a process like distillation or extraction.</term> and blended together—make only one concoction of only one and the same kind; so the *philosophers* rightly call their prepared matter "one and the same thing." This is true even though we are aware that it is a natural compound of certain substances from the same root and of the same kind, which together create one complete, uniform whole <note>original: "Homogeneity"</note>. In this sense, the *philosophers* all agree, though some

<meta>catchword: say,</meta>

<keywords>unity of matter, homogeneity, purification, superfluities, alchemical theory, Pyrophilus, Eudoxus</keywords>

say, their Matter is composed of two things, and others of three; though some write that it consists of four, and some of five; and others again, that it is but one single thing. They are all equally correct, because diverse things of the same kind naturally and intimately united—just as several waters distilled from herbs and mixed together indeed constitute only one and the same thing—and this is done in our Art. This is even more fundamental because the substances that make up the philosophical compound differ less from one another than sorrel water <note>A liquid extracted from the sorrel plant, often used in medicine and cooking for its acidic properties.</note> differs from lettuce water <note>A liquid distilled from lettuce, believed in early modern medicine to have cooling and sedative properties.</note>.

therefore work upon a liquid matter <note>Matter in a fluid or "volatile" state, as opposed to a solid body.</note> instead of a *stone*; please inform me, is this method the true one?

## EUDOXUS.

Your remark is very judicious; the majority of true *philosophers* share this author's opinion. The matter only needs to be dissolved and then coagulated. Mixing, conjunction, fixation, coagulation, and other similar operations happen almost by themselves, but solution is the great secret of the Art. It is this essential point that the *philosophers* do not reveal. Strictly speaking, all the operations of the "first work" <note>The initial stage of the alchemical process, often aimed at creating the "first medicine" or white elixir.</note> or the "first medicine" are nothing but a continual solution; thus, calcination, extraction, sublimation, and distillation are all, in truth, the solution of the matter. *Geber* <note>Abu Musa Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815), a famous polymath often regarded as the father of chemistry. His Latinized name is Geber.</note> did not teach the necessity of sublimation except because it not only purifies the matter from its coarse and combustible parts, but also because it prepares it for solution. From this comes the mercurial humidity <note>The "spirit" or volatile essence of the matter, often described as a metallic moisture or water that does not wet the hands.</note>, which is the key to the work.

<sig>D 3</sig>

<summary>Eudoxus explains that "solution"—the process of dissolving the matter—is the primary secret of alchemy, and that other operations like calcination and sublimation are simply different ways to achieve this solution.</summary>
<keywords>solution, coagulation, Geber, first work, first medicine, sublimation, mercurial humidity, alchemical operations</keywords>

Nevertheless, by the action of the natural fire, this matter should be dissolved into a "dry water" <note>An alchemical term for a substance that is liquid in potential or appearance but does not wet the hands, often associated with "mercurial water."</note> as easily as ice is melted by the slightest heat.

->*PYROPHILUS.*<-

I know very well that it is the principal key of the Art; many *philosophers* have described its nature through very obscure parables, but I confess to you that I have not yet been able to understand this mystery.

## E U D O X U S.

It is indeed the great mystery of the Art, as all other mysteries of this sublime philosophy depend on the knowledge of this. I would be very satisfied if I could plainly <note>original: "nakedly"</note> explain this secret to you; but I cannot do what no *philosopher* believes is within his power to do. All you can reasonably expect of me is to tell you that the natural fire which this *philosopher* mentions is a "potential fire" <note>A fire that exists in possibility or power—meaning a substance that acts like fire (by dissolving or transforming) without being a visible flame.</note> that does not burn the hands, but shows its effectiveness when it is slightly activated <note>original: "excited"</note> by an external fire. It is, therefore, a truly secret fire that this author calls *Lunar Vulcan* <note>original: "Lunar Vulcan." Vulcan was the Roman god of fire; "Lunar" implies a cold or "moist" fire, often associated with the alchemical process of the moon/silver stage.</note> in the title of his work. *Artephius* <note>A legendary alchemist, likely writing in the 12th century, famous for his "Secret Book" regarding the prolongation of life and the "Secret Fire."</note> has provided a more thorough description of it than any other *philosopher*. *Pontanus* <note>Johannes Pontanus, an alchemist who famously confessed to failing many times until he discovered the "Philosophic Fire."</note> has copied him and tells us that he failed two hundred times because he did not know this fire until he had read and understood *Artephius*. This mysterious fire is "natural" because it shares the same nature as the philosophical matter; nevertheless, the chemist <note>original: "Artist"</note> prepares them both.

# THE HERMETICAL TRIUMPH <note>The full title of the work is *The Hermetical Triumph*.</note>

matter <note>Continuing from the previous page: "it is evident that the matter..."</note> and subject of the Art must not be sought for, except in the family of metals and minerals, and that all who work upon other subjects are on the road to error.

->EUDOXUS.<-

I will answer that your conclusion is very well drawn. This *Philosopher* is not alone in speaking this way; he agrees in this with the greatest number of authorities, both ancient and modern. *Geber* <note>Abu Musa Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–c. 815), a famous chemist and alchemist often called the "Father of Chemistry."</note>, who perfectly understood the alchemical process <note>original: "Magistery"</note> and who used no allegory, treats throughout his entire *Summa* <note>original: "Summa Perfectionis" or "The Sum of Perfection," Geber's most influential work.</note> only of metals and minerals, of bodies and spirits, and of the right manner of preparing them to accomplish the Work. 

But as the philosophical matter is partly body and partly spirit—as in one sense it is terrestrial, and in another it is entirely celestial—and as some authors consider it in one sense while others treat it in another, this has caused the error of a great number of artists. These people, under the name of *Universalists*, reject every matter that has received a specific form <note>original: "determinative"</note> from nature. They do this because they cannot break down the specific matter to separate from it the grain, which is the pure universal substance that the specific matter hides in its core <note>original: "Bosom"</note>, and to which

# THE HERMETICAL TRIUMPH <note>The full title of the work is *The Hermetical Triumph*.</note>

the wise and enlightened alchemist <note>original: "illuminated Artist"</note> can completely provide all the universality necessary for it. understand so miraculous an Art. ### PYROPHILUS.

You give me a most heartfelt <note>original: "sensible"</note> joy. I have read much; I have meditated even more; I have exercised myself in the practice of chemistry <note>original: "Chymistry"</note>; I have verified the saying of *Artephius* <note>An influential 12th-century alchemist whose works, like "The Secret Book," were foundational to the Hermetic tradition.</note>, who assures us, *That he knows not the composition of metals, who is ignorant of how they must be destroyed; and without this destruction, it is impossible to extract the metallic humidity <note>The "metallic humidity" refers to the liquid essence or "mercury" of the metal, which alchemists believed must be released by breaking down the solid form.</note>, which is the true Key of the Art*. 

Therefore, I can assure myself that I have acquired the greatest part of the qualities that, according to you, are required in one who aspires to this high understanding. I have, besides, a very particular advantage: the kindness you show in being willing to communicate part of your knowledge to me by clearing up my doubts. Permit me, therefore, to go on and ask you: upon what foundation does *Gold* use such outrageous terms toward the *Philosophers' Stone*, (*) calling it a *venomous worm* and treating it with the name <note>original: "Appellation"</note> of *Enemy of Men and of Metals*?

### EUDOXUS.

It makes the perfect more than perfect, and fit to make the imperfect perfect.

### EUDOXUS.

->E U D O X U S.<-

There is a great deal of difference between declaring positively that a certain matter is not the true Subject of the Art—as they do regarding Gold <term>☉</term> <note>The symbol for the Sun, representing gold.</note> and Copper <term>♀</term> <note>The symbol for the planet Venus, representing copper.</note>—and giving the "Children of the Science" <note>A traditional term for dedicated students or initiates of alchemy.</note> an understanding of the most important secrets through figures and allegories. 

Those students have the advantage of clearly discerning philosophical truths through the enigmatic veils <note>original: enigmetical vails</note> with which the Sages so artfully cover and conceal them. In the first case, the Philosophers speak the truth negatively, without any double meaning. However, when they speak both affirmatively and clearly upon this subject, it can be concluded that those who stick to the literal sense of their words will undoubtedly be deceived. 

The *Philosophers* have no more certain way to hide their science from the unworthy, and open it to the wise, than to explain it by allegories in the essential points of their Art. It was this that made *Artephius* <note>Artephius (c. 1150) was a famous alchemist to whom several influential texts were attributed; legend claimed he lived for over a thousand years.</note> say, *That this Art is entirely Cabalistical* <note>original: Cabalistical; here meaning a secret or mystical tradition that requires a specific key or divine inspiration to decode.</note>, for the understanding of which, there is need of a kind of revelation—

It is only the *Philosopher* <note>In this context, the "Philosopher" is the alchemist or practitioner of the Hermetic arts.</note> who can carry Nature from an unfinished imperfection even to a state more than perfect. It is therefore necessary that our mastery <note>original: "Magistery," referring to the alchemical process or the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> produce a "more than perfection" <note>original: "plusquam Perfection"</note>. To accomplish this, the sage must begin with an imperfect thing which, being on the path toward perfection, is found to have a natural disposition to be carried even to a state of "more than perfection" by the help of an art that is wholly divine—an art which is able to exceed the limited bounds of Nature. Indeed, if our art could not elevate a subject to a state of "more than perfection," neither could we give perfection to what is imperfect, and all our philosophy would be in vain.

It is a constant truth that the copulation of the male and of the female is ordained by Nature for the generation of animals. ->EUDOXUS.<-

There is no difficulty in giving you satisfaction regarding this point. In fact, I wonder how you came to form such a doubt. The Stone <note>The "Philosopher's Stone," the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold.</note> is the "first matter" of the metals, and consequently, it exists prior to Gold <note>original: ☉, the alchemical symbol for the Sun or Gold</note> and to all other metals. Even if it derives its origin from them, or if it is born from their destruction, it does not follow that it is a product created after metals. On the contrary, it is prior to them, since it is the matter from which all metals were originally formed. The secret of the Art <note>Alchemy was frequently referred to simply as "the Art."</note> consists in knowing how to extract from metals this first matter—this metallic germ—which is meant to grow through the fertility of the "Philosophic Sea." <note>A metaphorical term for the universal solvent or mercurial starting material used in the alchemical process.</note>

->PYROPHILUS.<-

I am now convinced of this truth, and I find Gold to be inexcusable for being so lacking in respect toward its elder. The Stone has on its side the most ancient and the greatest *Philosophers*. *Hermes*, *Plato*, and *Aristotle* are all in its favor, and everyone knows them to be unquestionable judges in this matter. Permit me only to ask you a question regarding each of the passages cited from those *Philosophers*.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the philosophical "Stone" and its origins in Hermetic tradition, specifically referencing the Emerald Tablet.</meta>

# The HERMETICAL

whom the Stone has here cited to prove by their authority that it is the only and true matter of the wise men <note>original: "wise Men," referring to the Alchemists or Magi</note>.

The passage of the Emerald Table <note>original: "smaragdine Table," the Tabula Smaragdina, a foundational Hermetic text</note> of the great *Hermes* proves the excellency of the Stone, in that it shows that the Stone is endowed with two natures: that is, with the nature of superior beings and with that of inferior beings. It shows that these two natures both alike have one and the same origin; so that we must conclude that they (being perfectly united in the Stone) compose a third being of an inexpressible virtue. 

But I know not whether you will agree with my sentiments regarding the translation of this passage and the commentary of *Hortulanus* <note>a 14th-century alchemist known for his commentary on the Emerald Tablet</note>. One reads after these words: *"That which is below is as that which is on high; and that which is on high is as that which is below."* One reads (I say): <margin>25.</margin> *"To do the miracles of one only thing."* 

For my part, I believe that the *Latin* original <note>original: "quod est inferius, est sicut quod est superius... ad penetranda miracula rei unius"</note> has quite another sense; for the word *by which* <note>original: "quibus"</note>, which connects the last words with the preceding ones, signifies: *"That by these things"* (that is to say, by the union of these two natures) *"one performs the miracles of one thing."* The word "to," which the translator and commentator use, destroys the sense and the reasoning of the passage.

<summary>The author analyzes the Emerald Tablet of Hermes Trismegistus, arguing that the "Stone" derives its power from the perfect union of celestial and terrestrial natures. He offers a grammatical correction to existing Latin translations to clarify how this union enables alchemical miracles.</summary>

<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Emerald Tablet, Alchemical Stone, Hortulanus, Hermetic Philosophy, Macrocosm and Microcosm, Latin translation</keywords>

Have the goodness, if you please, to tell me how <margin>26.</margin> you understand this: (*) *"It weds itself, it is with child by itself, and it is born of itself."* <note>original: "Ipsa se sponsat, ipsa se imprægnat, ipsa se parit." ->EUDOXUS<-

*The Stone weds itself* insofar as in its first generation, it is Nature alone, assisted by Art, that makes the perfect union of the two substances which give it being. From this union, there results at the same time the essential purification <note>original: "depuration"</note> of the metallic *Sulphur* and *Mercury* <note>In alchemy, Sulphur and Mercury represent the active/masculine and passive/feminine principles of matter, rather than the common chemical elements.</note>. This is a union and marriage so natural that the artist, who lends his hands to it by disposing all things required, can give no demonstration of it by the rules of Art; since he cannot even so much as well comprehend the mystery of this union.

<summary>Eudoxus explains a paradoxical alchemical riddle, arguing that the Philosopher's Stone is self-generating through a natural union of Sulphur and Mercury that transcends human understanding.</summary>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue regarding the alchemical "Stone," focusing on its self-generative properties and its symbolic transformation into the Universal Medicine.</meta>

# THE TRIUMPH

*The Stone is pregnant by itself*; when Art, continuing to assist Nature by purely natural means, puts the Stone in the necessary state to impregnate itself with the Astral Seed <note>The "Astral Seed" refers to a celestial or cosmic influence that alchemists believed descended from the stars to give life to matter.</note>. This renders it fruitful and gives it the power of multiplying its own kind.

*The Stone is born of itself*; because after having wedded itself, and after being pregnant by itself—with Art doing nothing more than assisting Nature through the maintenance of the heat necessary for generation—it takes a new birth from itself. This is just as the *Phoenix* is born again from its own ashes. It becomes the "Son of the Sun," the universal medicine for all living things, and the true living gold of the *Philosophers*. Through the continued help of Art and the service of the Artist, it acquires in a short time the Royal Diadem <note>A crown or symbol of sovereignty, representing the Stone reaching its highest state of perfection.</note> and sovereign power over all its brethren.

It is this concept that leads *Cosmopolite* <note>A pseudonym for Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a famous alchemist</note> to say in his *Enigma* that in the delicious island he describes, there was no water. He notes that all the water people attempted to bring there by machines or by art *was either useless or poisoned, except for that which a few people knew how to extract from the rays of the Sun or the Moon.* 

The method for making this water descend from heaven is truly wonderful. It is found in the Stone, which contains the "central water." This is indeed one and the same thing as the "celestial water." However, the secret consists in knowing how to make the Stone act as a magnet to attract, embrace, and unite this astral quintessence <note>term: the purest, fifth element derived from the stars</note> to itself. In this way, they form a single essence that is perfect—and more than perfect—able to grant perfection to imperfect things once the Magistery <note>term: the completion of the "Great Work" or the alchemical process</note> is accomplished.

->EUDOXUS.<-

Note that there are two errors in your reasoning. The first is that you suppose that common **mercury** <note>original: "Quicksilver"</note> is the first and sole matter from which metals are formed in the mines, which is not so. **Mercury** is a metal which, having less **sulphur** <note>In alchemy, sulphur represents the principle of combustibility and structure, while mercury represents liquidity and volatility.</note> and less earthy impurity than other metals, remains liquid and flowing. It unites with metals, and particularly with **gold**, as gold is the purest of all; it unites less easily with other metals in proportion to how impure they are in their natural composition. 

You must therefore know that there is a "first matter" <term>materia prima</term> of metals, from which **mercury** itself is formed. It is a viscous and mercurial water, which is the "Water of our Stone" <note>A reference to the Elixir or Philosopher's Stone in its liquid state.</note>. And this is the opinion of the true **philosophers** <note>original: "Philosophers," referring here to alchemists or natural philosophers.</note>.

But you must consider that *mercury* is not called a "spirit" by *Geber* except because it flees from fire, due to the mobility of its uniform substance. Nevertheless, that does not properly hinder it from being a metallic body. For that reason, it can never be so perfectly united with another metal that it won't always be separated from it whenever it finds itself pressed by the action of fire. Experience proves this reasoning to be true; therefore, the *Stone* <note>The Philosopher's Stone, personified here as a speaker in the dialogue</note> is right to maintain against *Gold* that there can never be a perfect union between gold and common mercury.

# The HERMETICAL

Recollect yourself: if the Body of the Stone were not imperfect with an imperfection—one in which, nevertheless, Nature has not yet finished her operation—one could not seek for perfection there, and much less would one be able to find it. This being established, it will be very easy for you to judge that the constancy of the Soul and the perfection of the Tincture <note>Tincture: In alchemy, a spiritualized substance or "medicine" capable of transmuting base metals into gold or curing disease.</note> are not actually present, nor in a state to manifest themselves in the Stone, so long as it remains in its imperfect being. 

But then, when by the continuance of the Work, the substance of the Stone has passed from imperfection to perfection, and from perfection to *more than* perfection <note>original: "plusquam perfection"</note>, the constancy of its Soul and the efficacy of the Tincture of its Spirit are brought from potentiality into Act. Thus, the Soul, the Spirit, and the Body of the Stone, being equally exalted, compose one whole thing of a nature and of a virtue that is incomprehensible.

->*PYROPHILUS*<-

<margin>33.</margin>

->EUDOXUS.<-

And yet, I hope to reconcile you quickly to it. This *Philosopher* is not the only one who uses this sort of language; the greater part of them speak after the same manner as he does. To tell you the truth, our Stone is properly *invisible*, as well in regard to its matter as in regard to its form. 

It is invisible in regard to its matter because, although our Stone, or our *Mercury* <note>In alchemy, "Mercury" often refers to the "mercury of the philosophers," a foundational substance rather than common quicksilver.</note> (for there is no difference) does really exist, it is yet true that it does not appear to our eyes—at least, not unless the artist lends his hand to Nature to help her bring forth this philosophical production. This made *Cosmopolite* <note>original: "Cosmopolite"; likely referring to Michael Sendivogius, a famous 17th-century alchemist who wrote under the pseudonym 'The Cosmopolite.'</note> say, *That the subject of our philosophy has a real existence; but that it is not to be seen, except when it pleases the artist to make it appear.*

The Stone is likewise no less invisible in regard to its form. I call its "form" here the principle of its admirable faculties; for

It does not manifest itself except by the surprising effects it produces. The philosophers often speak of their Stone when considered in that sense. **Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom the "Hermetic" writings are attributed.</note> understands it this way when he says, *"The wind carries it in its belly"* <note>original: "Vento portavit illud in ventre suo" from the Emerald Tablet.</note>; and **Cosmopolite** <note>Michael Sendivogius, a famous 17th-century alchemist who wrote under the name "Cosmopolita."</note> is not far from this Father of Philosophy when he assures us, *"That our subject is before the eyes of all the world; that no one can live without it; that all creatures make use of it; but that few perceive it."* Well then, do you not agree with your author’s sentiment? Must you not confess that in whatever manner you consider the Stone, it is truly said to be invisible?

->EUDOXUS.<-

There is certainly none more fundamental, since this *Philosopher* tells you in this passage how the Stone is formed, upon which all our philosophy is founded. Our *Mercury* or our Stone does indeed take birth from two bodies; but note, that it is not the simple mixture of two bodies which produces our *Mercury* or our Stone. For I have just shown you that bodies are contraries, and there can be no perfect union made of them. Our Stone, on the contrary, is born from the destruction of two bodies, which act one upon the other—as the male and the female, or as the body and the spirit—in a manner no less natural than it is incomprehensible to the artist who assists it.

And to refine a Matter that is in itself unclean and gross. This is a most important truth that deserves your careful reflection.

Remark that to attain this end, many operations are required. Because these all tend toward one and the same goal, the *Philosophers* <note>The "Philosophers" refers to historical alchemists.</note> generally consider them as only one single operation continued in different ways. Observe that fire first separates the heterogeneous <note>original: "heterogenous"; meaning parts that are different in kind or nature.</note> parts and joins together the homogeneous parts of our Stone. 

The secret fire afterwards produces the same effect, but more efficaciously, by introducing into the Matter a fiery spirit. This spirit opens the internal secret gate, which refines and sublimes the pure parts, separating them from those that are terrestrial and combustible <note>original: "aduſtible"; capable of being burned or scorched.</note>. 

The solution, which is afterwards made by the addition of the astral *Quintessence* <note>The "fifth element" or purest essence of a substance.</note> which animates the Stone, performs a third purification <note>original: "Depuration"</note> of it, and distillation completes it entirely. Thus, by purifying and refining the Stone through many different degrees—to which the Philosophers usually give the names of several different operations and the "Conversion of Elements"—it is exalted to that perfection which is the nearest state to conduct it to *more than* perfection <note>original: "plusquam perfection"</note> by a

# THE TRIUMPH.

The regimen is proportioned to the final intention of the Art, that is to say, unto perfect fixation <note>original: "Fixtion"; the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat</note>. You see now that, to speak properly, there is but one way, just as there is but one intention in the Great Work. The *Philosophers* do not describe many ways; rather, they consider the different **degrees** of purification <note>original: "Depurations"</note> as so many separate operations and different ways. They do this with the design (as your author very well remarks) to conceal this admirable Art.

As for the words by which your author concludes—that is, that the solution of the body is only made in its own blood—I must have you observe that in our Art, there are three essential solutions made at three different times. In these, the body is not dissolved except in its own blood, and that occurs in the beginning, in the middle, and at the end of the work; take good notice of this. 

I have already shown you that in the principal operations of the Art, there are always two things which produce one. Of those two things, one supplies the place of the **Male**, and the other of the **Female**; one is the Body, the other is the **Spirit**. You must apply that principle here: specifically, that in the three solutions I mentioned to you,

This is the order of these three important dissolutions.

You would attempt in vain to achieve the true dissolution of the Male in the first operation by using Fire alone; you could never succeed without the conjunction of the Female. It is in their mutual embraces that they merge <note>original: "confound"</note> and change each other to produce a single, uniform substance <note>original: "Homogeneity"</note> that is different from them both. # The HERMETICAL <note>original: "The HERMETICAL [Triumph]"</note>

It surpasses indeed all the imperfect metals, transmuting them into perfect metals in such a manner that there remains no trace of what they were before.

->PYROPHILUS<-

I very well comprehend these reasons, and yet I still have one remaining doubt touching perfect metals; *Gold*, for example, is a metal constant and perfect, which, therefore, it seems to me, the Stone should not be able to devour.

->EUDOXUS<-

Your doubt is groundless; for as the Stone, strictly speaking, does not devour imperfect metals, but so changes their nature that there remains nothing to identify what they were before; so the Stone, while not being able to destroy *Gold*, nor to transmute it into a more perfect metal, transmutes it into a Medicine a thousand times more perfect than *Gold*. This is because it can then transmute a thousand times as much imperfect metal, according to the degree of perfection that the Stone has received from the Art <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy or the Great Work.</note>.

### PYROPHILUS.

I perceive now how little foundation there was for my doubt. To be plain, there is so much subtlety in even the smallest hints of the *Philosophers* <note>original: "Philosophers," referring here to the ancient and medieval alchemists</note> that you should not think it strange that I have often been stuck on things that otherwise should have appeared sufficiently intelligible by themselves. 

I have only two more questions to ask you. These concern the two pieces of advice that my author gives to the "Sons of the Science" <note>A traditional term for students or practitioners of alchemy</note> regarding the manner of proceeding and the goal they should set for themselves in their search for the universal *Medicine*. 

He advises them, in the first place, to sharpen their minds, to read the writings of the wise men with prudence, and to labor with exactness. He warns them to proceed without haste in a work so precious because, as he says: 

<margin>39.</margin> 
*It has its time ordained by Nature, just like the fruits upon the trees and the clusters of grapes borne by the vine.* 

I understand the usefulness of this counsel well enough; but please, be so kind as to explain to me how this limitation of time is to be understood.

<summary>Pyrophilus acknowledges the complexity of alchemical writings and asks for clarification on the "ordained time" required by Nature to complete the medicinal Great Work.</summary>

->EUDOXUS.<-

Your Author sufficiently explains it to you by the comparison of fruits, which Nature produces in their due season; this comparison is exact. The Stone <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the ultimate goal of alchemy.</note> is a field which the wise man cultivates, into which Art and Nature have placed the seed that is to produce its fruit. And just as the four seasons of the year are necessary for the perfect production of fruits, so the Stone has, in like manner, its determined seasons. 

It has its **Winter**, during which cold and humidity hold dominion in this earth, which has been thus prepared and sown. It has its **Spring**, wherein the philosophical seed, being warmed, gives signs of vegetation and increase. It has its **Summer**, during which its fruit ripens and becomes suitable for multiplication <note>The process of increasing the power or quantity of the alchemical medicine.</note>. And it has its **Autumn**, in which this fruit, being perfectly ripe, rejoices the wise who have the good fortune to gather it.

To leave you nothing to desire upon this subject, I must here make you observe three things. *First*, that the Sage <term>Sage</term><note>original: "Sage"; a term for an experienced and enlightened alchemist.</note> ought to imitate Nature in the practice of the Work; and as this wise worker (namely, Nature) can produce nothing perfect if its motion is made violent, so the Artist ought to suffer

# TRIUMPH

the principles of his matter to act internally, by externally administering a warmth or heat proportioned to its need. The second thing is that the knowledge of the four seasons of the Work <note>The "Work" refers to the alchemical process of transmutation or the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> ought to be a rule which the wise should follow in the different regimens <note>original: "Regiments"; here meaning the systematic management or regulation of the fire's intensity.</note> of the fire, proportioning it to each stage just as Nature shows us. Nature requires less heat to put the trees in blossom and to form the fruit than she does to make them perfectly ripe. *Thirdly*, although the Work has its four seasons just as Nature has, it does not follow that the seasons of Art <note>In this context, "Art" refers to the alchemist's laboratory work as a mirror of natural processes.</note> and of Nature must precisely correspond to each other. The "summer" of the Work may happen without any problem during Nature’s autumn, and its "autumn" during her winter. It is sufficient that the regulation of the fire be proportioned to the season of the Work; it is in that alone that the great secret of the regulation consists, for which I cannot give you a more certain rule.

My author, in the second place, advises the <term>Sons of Art</term> <note>A traditional term for alchemists or initiates into the Hermetic mysteries</note> to have uprightness of heart and to propose an honest end for themselves in this work. ## EUDOXUS.

You are sufficiently instructed in our *Philosophy* to comprehend that the possession of the universal *Medicine* <note>The "Universal Medicine" or "Panacea" was believed by alchemists to cure all diseases and prolong life.</note> and of the great *Elixir* is, of all worldly treasures, the greatest, the most real, and the most valuable that man can enjoy. Indeed, immense *riches*, supreme *dignities*, and all the *greatness* of the earth are not comparable to this precious treasure, which is the only temporal *good* able to satisfy the heart of man. It gives to him who possesses it a long life, exempt from all sorts of infirmities, and puts into his power more *gold* and *silver* than is possessed by all the most mighty *monarchs* together. This treasure has, moreover, this particular advantage above all other blessings of this life: that he who enjoys it finds himself perfectly satisfied even with its contemplation alone, and that he can never be troubled with the fear of losing it.

You are likewise fully convinced that God governs the world; that his divine *Providence* ensures that there reigns an *order* which his infinite *wisdom* has established in it from the beginning of all ages; and that this *Providence* is not

<summary>Eudoxus explains that the Alchemical Elixir is the greatest of all earthly treasures, providing health, wealth, and a unique sense of security and satisfaction. He begins to frame these alchemical achievements within the context of God's divine providence and universal order.</summary>
