# The HERMETICAL

that blind fatality of the ancients <note>original: "Antients"</note>, nor that pretended chain of causes, or that necessary order of things that must make events follow without any distinction. But on the contrary, you are well persuaded that the wisdom of God presides over all the events that happen or appear in the world.

Upon the double foundation which these two reflections establish, you cannot doubt but that God—who disposes sovereignly of all the possessions in the world—never permits those who apply themselves to the search of this precious treasure <note>The "precious treasure" refers to the Philosopher's Stone or the secret of transmutation.</note> with the design to make a bad use of it, to come into its possession through their endeavors. Really, what mischiefs might not be caused in the world by a perverse spirit who would have no other aim but to gratify his ambition and to indulge his lusts, if he had in his power and possession this certain means of executing his most criminal enterprises?

Therefore <note>original: "Wherefore"</note> the philosophers, who perfectly know what mischiefs and disorders might accrue to civil society if the knowledge of this great secret were revealed to the impious, do not treat of it except with fear, nor speak of it except enigmatically <note>term: enigmatically; speaking in riddles or metaphors to hide the true meaning from the unworthy.</note>, to the end that it may not be comprehended, except by

<summary>The author argues that God's providence prevents wicked individuals from discovering the secret of alchemy to ensure it is not used for ambitious or criminal purposes. Consequently, true philosophers only write about the secret in riddles to protect society.</summary>

There can be none who possess good sense and who fear God but must agree with these sentiments. It only remains for me to return you most humble thanks, for you have been pleased to treat me as a Son of the Science <note>original: "Son of the Science"; a term for an initiated or dedicated student of alchemy</note>, to discourse with me sincerely, and to instruct me in such great mysteries as clearly and as intelligibly as is lawful to do, and as I could reasonably desire. I protest to you that my gratitude shall last as long as my life.

IF I wrote this Letter to persuade those of the Truth of our Philosophy who imagine that it is only a vain Idea and a mere Paradox, I would follow the example of many Masters in this great Art <note>original: "Art"; here referring to Alchemy or the "Great Work" of transmutation.</note>; I would endeavor to convince those

# THE TRIUMPH

I would have corrected the errors of those sorts of wits by demonstrating to them the solidity of the principles of our science—which is supported by the laws and operations of Nature—and I would have spoken only briefly regarding what belongs to the practice. But as I have a quite different design, and because I write only for you, the wise disciples of *Hermes* <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy</note> and true Sons of the Art <note>original: "Sons of the Art"; a traditional term for initiated alchemists</note>, my only intention is to serve you as a guide in a way so difficult to follow. 

Our practice is, in effect, a track in the sands, where one ought to conduct oneself rather by the North Star than by any footsteps which are seen imprinted there. The confusion of the tracks, which an almost infinite number of people have left there, is so great, and one finds so many different paths—almost all of which lead into most frightful deserts—that it is almost impossible not to stray from the true road, which only the sages favored by Heaven have happily known how to find and discover.

This confusion stops the Sons of Art at once; some in the beginning, others in the middle of this philosophical course, and some even when they approach near the end of this painful journey.

<summary>The author explains that his goal is to guide initiated alchemists through the practical "Great Work," comparing the process to navigating a trackless desert where one must rely on celestial guidance rather than following the failed paths of others.</summary>

I vow sincerely to you, that the practice of our art <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy or the Hermetic Science.</note> is the most difficult thing in the world—not in regard to its physical operations, but in respect of the difficulties found in learning it clearly from the books of the philosophers. For if on one side it is called with good reason the *play of children* <note>original: "ludus puerorum"; a common alchemical phrase suggesting that once the secret is known, the process is simple and effortless.</note>; on the other, it requires in those who search for the truth by their labor and their study a profound knowledge of the principles and of the operations of nature in the three kinds <note>The "three kinds" refers to the three kingdoms of nature: animal, vegetable, and mineral.</note>; but particularly in the mineral and metallic kind. our Art; the rest of the Work is found clearly enough taught in the Books of the *Philosophers*, so that we have no need of a more ample explanation.

As there are three Kingdoms <note>The three kingdoms of nature: Animal, Vegetable, and Mineral.</note> in Nature, so there are also three Medicines in our Art which make three different works in the practice. These are nevertheless but three different degrees, which raise our Elixir to its highest perfection. I will put into your hands six keys, with which you may enter into the sanctuary of philosophy, open all its recesses, and arrive at the understanding of the most hidden truths.

<header>TRIUMPH.</header>

## The First Key

The first Key is that which opens the dark prisons in which the *Sulphur* <note>In alchemy, Sulphur represents the active, male principle or the "soul" of a substance.</note> is shut up. This is the tool that knows how to extract the Seed out of the Body, and which forms the Stone of the *Philosophers* <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life.</note> by the conjunction of the Male with the Female; of the Spirit with the Body; and of *Sulphur* with *Mercury* <note>Mercury represents the passive, female principle or the "spirit."</note>. *Hermes* <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical founder of alchemy.</note> has clearly demonstrated the operation of this first Key with these words:

> *In the caverns of the metals, the Stone is hidden; it is venerable, bright in color, a sublime mind, and an open sea.* <note>original Latin: "In cavernis metallorum occultatur Lapis, qui est venerabilis, colore splendidus, mens sublimis, et mare apertum."</note>

This Stone has a bright glittering; it contains a spirit of a sublime origin; it is the "Sea of the Wise" in which they fish for their mysterious fish. The same philosopher takes even more particular notice of the birth <note>original: "Nativity"</note> of this admirable Stone when he says:

> *The King shall come out of the fire and shall rejoice in his marriage, and the hidden things shall be revealed.* <note>original Latin: "Exeat Rex ab igne, et gaudeat conjugio, et occulta patebunt."</note>

It is a King crowned with glory who has his birth in the fire, and who is pleased with the union of the spouse who is given to him. It is this union which makes manifest that which was previously hidden.

<meta>catchword: But</meta>

<summary>This page describes the "First Key" of alchemy, which involves releasing the "Sulphur" from its physical prison to begin the creation of the Philosopher's Stone. It emphasizes the symbolic marriage of male and female principles as the foundation of the Great Work.</summary>

# The HERMETICAL

But before I go any further, I have some counsel to give you, which will be of no small advantage to you. That is, to reflect on the fact that the operations of each of the three Works <note>The "three works" usually refer to the purification, white, and red stages of alchemy.</note> have a great deal of analogy and relation to one another. Because of this, the *Philosophers* <note>original: "Philosophers"; referring here to alchemists and natural philosophers.</note> designedly speak in equivocal terms, so that those who do not have *lynx-like eyes* <note>meaning exceptionally sharp or penetrating vision</note> may pursue the wrong path and be lost in this labyrinth, from which it is very hard to escape. 

In effect, when one imagines that they are speaking of one stage of the Work, they are often treating another. Take heed, therefore, not to be deceived in this. For it is a truth that in each stage of the Work, the wise artist ought to dissolve the body with the spirit; he must cut off the *Raven’s Head* <note>A term for "caput mortuum" or the black, decayed matter at the start of the process.</note>, whiten the black, and turn the white to red. Yet it is properly in the first operation that the wise artist cuts off the head of the black *Dragon* and of the *Raven*. *Hermes* <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy.</note> says that it is from there that our Art takes its beginning: *What is born of the Crow is the beginning of this Art.* <note>original Latin: "Quod nascitur ex Corvo, est principium hujus Artis."</note> 

Consider that it is by the separation of the black, foul, and stinking fume of the blackest black, that our astral, white, and resplendent Stone is formed. This Stone contains in its veins the blood of the Pelican <note>In alchemy, the Pelican symbolizes the stage of "multiplication" or "feeding" the stone with its own essence, much like the myth of the bird feeding its young with its own blood.</note>;

<summary>The author warns the reader that alchemical texts are intentionally deceptive and explains that the first stage of the Work involves purifying the "blackest black" to find the resplendent Stone.</summary>

Pelican <note>A "Pelican" is a specific type of circulating alchemical flask with tubes that return distilled liquid to the body of the vessel.</note>; it is at this first purification of the Stone, and at this shining whiteness, that the first key of the first work is ended.

### *The Second Key.*

The second key dissolves the compound, or the Stone, and begins the separation of the elements in a philosophical manner. This separation of the elements is not achieved except by raising up the subtle and pure parts above the thick and terrestrial parts. He who knows how to sublime <note>original: "sublime"; in alchemy, this refers to the process of heating a substance until it vaporizes and then solidifies again, used here to describe spiritual or physical refinement.</note> the Stone philosophically, justly deserves the name of a *Philosopher*, since he knows the "Fire of the Wise," which is the only instrument that can perform this sublimation. 

No *Philosopher* has ever openly revealed this secret fire and this powerful agent, which performs all the wonders of the Art. He who does not understand it, and does not know how to distinguish it by the characteristics with which I have endeavored to point it out in the discourse of *Eudoxus* and *Pyrophilus* <note>Eudoxus and Pyrophilus are the two characters in the dialogue of this book; Eudoxus acts as the teacher and Pyrophilus as the student.</note>, ought to stop here and pray to God to make it clear to him. For the knowledge of this great secret is a gift of Heaven, rather than a light acquired by the force of reasoning. Let him, nevertheless, read the writings

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Without the Sublimation <note>original: "Sublimation"; the process of heating a substance until it vaporizes and then cools back into a solid to purify it</note> of the Stone, the Conversion of the Elements and the Extraction of the Principles is impossible. This conversion—which makes Water out of Earth, Air out of Water, and Fire out of Air—is the only way by which our *Mercury* can be prepared. Apply yourself, then, to know this secret Fire, which dissolves the Stone naturally and without violence. It causes the Stone to dissolve into water in the "Great Sea of the *Wise*" through the distillation performed by the rays of the Sun and of the Moon. 

It is in this manner that the Stone, which according to *Hermes* <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom the foundation of alchemy is attributed</note> is the "Vine of the *Wise*," becomes their Wine. This Father of our Philosophy cries out concerning this *Mystery*:

### *The third Key.*

The third Key comprises within itself a longer series of operations than all the rest combined. The *Philosophers* <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers to alchemists or seekers of natural wisdom.</note> have spoken very little of it, because the perfection of our *Mercury* <note>term: Mercury; here referring to the "Philosophical Mercury," the essential animated fluid or "soul" of metals, rather than common quicksilver.</note> depends upon it. Even the most sincere authors, such as *Artephius*, *Trevisan*, and *Flamel*, have passed over the preparations of our *Mercury* in silence. There is hardly one to be found who has not used deception instead of showing the longest and most important operations of our practice. 

With the intention of lending you a hand in this part of the journey—where, for want of light, it is impossible to follow the true road—I will speak more extensively on this third Key than the *Philosophers* have done. At the very least, I will present in an orderly fashion what they have said on this subject so confusedly that, without the inspiration of Heaven or the help of a faithful friend, one undoubtedly remains lost in this labyrinth without being able to find a happy escape. I am sure that you, who are the true Sons of the Science, will receive very great satisfaction—

<summary>The author introduces the third Key of alchemy, noting its complexity and the intentional secrecy surrounding it in historical texts, promising a clearer explanation of the preparation of Philosophical Mercury.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the explanation of the chemical and spiritual processes involved in the "Hermetical Key," focusing on the transformation of the philosophical Mercury.</meta>

# The HERMETICAL

on in the explaining of these hidden mysteries, which regard the separation and the purification of the principles of our Mercury. This is achieved by a perfect dissolution and glorification of the body from which it had its birth, and by the intimate union of the soul with its body. The spirit is the only tie which works this conjunction; this is the intention and the essential point of the operations of this Key, which end in the generation of a new substance, infinitely nobler than the first.

After the wise artist has made a spring of living water come out of the stone, has pressed out the juice of the vine of the *Philosophers*, and has made their wine, he ought to take notice that in this homogenous substance—which appears in the form of water—there are three different substances and three natural principles of all bodies: *Salt, Sulphur,* and *Mercury*. These are the spirit, the soul, and the body. Although they appear pure and perfectly united together, they are still far from being truly so; for when we draw the water (which is the soul and the spirit) by distillation, the body remains at the bottom of the vessel.

...like a dead, black, and dreggy earth, which nevertheless is not to be despised; for in our <term>Subject</term><note>The "Subject" refers to the raw material or "Prima Materia" used at the start of the alchemical Great Work.</note> there is nothing which is not good. This advice opens the eyes of those who, seeking to make an exact purification of the <term>Elements</term> and of the <term>Principles</term>, persuade themselves that they must only take the subtle parts and cast away the heavy. But the Sons of the Science<note>A traditional term for alchemists or students of the "Hermetic" arts.</note> ought not to be ignorant that the <term>Fire</term> and the <term>Sulphur</term> are hidden in the center of the earth. This is the essential mystery of this operation, which is not accomplished until after a convenient digestion and a slow distillation. Follow then, you Sons of Art, the command which the truth-telling *Hermes*<note>Refers to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom the foundation of alchemy is attributed.</note> gives you, who says in this place: *But with this watery soul...*

*We must mix our Vinegar <note>original: "Vinegar"; likely referring to the universal solvent or acetum philosophorum</note>, so that we may possess the sulphurous Form; for when the Compound is dissolved, it is the Key of Restoration.* You know that nothing is more contrary than Fire and Water; but yet the wise Artist must make peace between these enemies, who at their core [or fundamentally] love one another vehemently. *Cosmopolite* <note>A pseudonym for Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a famous alchemist.</note> has described the method in a few words: *Therefore, once the things are purged, make Fire and Water to be friends, which they will easily do in their Earth which had ascended with them.* 

Be attentive then on this point: moisten the Earth often with its Water, and you will obtain what you seek. # The HERMETICAL

*the greatest Philosophy, and the Secret of all Secrets.*

But so that you may not be deceived by the term "Compound," I will tell you that the *Philosophers* <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers to Alchemists.</note> recognize two sorts of compounds. 

The first is the **Compound of Nature**. This is what I spoke of in the first Key; for it is Nature that creates it in a manner incomprehensible to the Artist <note>The "Artist" is the alchemist or practitioner.</note>, who does nothing but lend a hand to Nature by the application of external things. By these means, Nature brings forth and produces this admirable compound. 

The second is the **Compound of Art**. This is made by the wise man through the secret union of the fixed with the volatile, perfectly joined with all the prudence that can be acquired by the insights of a profound philosophy. The Compound of Art is not exactly the same in the second stage of the work as it is in the third; yet it is always the Artist who creates it. 

*Geber* <note>An influential 8th-century Islamic alchemist and polymath, known in Latin as Geber.</note> defines it as a mixture of *quicksilver* <note>original: "Argent vive"</note> and *sulfur*—that is to say, a mixture of the volatile and the fixed. By acting upon one another, they are reciprocally volatilized and fixed until they reach a state of perfect stability. Consider the example of Nature: you will see that the Earth will never produce fruit if it is not penetrated—

# THE TRIUMPH

It is like ice, and is melted with heat like butter. *Hermes* <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy.</note> calls it the "foliated earth" or the "earth of leaves," and with good reason; for if you observe it closely, it is entirely composed of layers or leaves. In a word, it is the most clear fountain mentioned by *Count Trevisan* <note>Bernard Trevisan (1406–1490), an Italian alchemist who wrote on the "Natural Philosophy of Metals."</note>. It is the true permanent water, which, after having radically dissolved the metals, is inseparably united to them and increases both their weight and their tincture <note>term: Tincture; in alchemy, the spiritual principle or "dye" capable of transmuting base metals into gold.</note>.

### The Fourth Key

The fourth key of the art is the entrance into the second work. It is the process that reduces our "water" into "earth." There is only this one water in the world that can be converted into earth by simple boiling, because the *Mercury of the Wise* carries its own *Sulphur* <note>In alchemy, Mercury represents the fluid/passive principle, while Sulphur represents the active/combustible principle.</note> in its center, which causes it to coagulate. The "terrification of the spirit" <note>term: Terrification; the process of turning a spiritual or liquid substance into a solid, earthy state.</note> is the only operation of this work. Therefore, boil with patience. If you have proceeded correctly, you will not go long without seeing the signs of this coagulation. If these signs do not appear in their proper time, they will never appear; because it is an undoubted sign that you have failed in some essential...

# The HERMETICAL

essential thing in the first operations; for to corporify <note>original: "corporify"; to give a spiritual substance a physical body</note> the spirit, which is our *Mercury*, you must have well dissolved the body in which the *Sulphur*—which coagulates the *Mercury*—is enclosed. *Hermes* <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of Hermeticism</note> assures us that our mercurial water shall obtain all the virtues which the *Philosophers* attribute to it when it shall be changed into earth: *Its force will be entire, if it shall be converted into earth* <note>original Latin: "Vis ejus integra est, si versa fuerit in terram," from the Emerald Tablet</note>. This is an earth admirable for its fertility: the "Land of Promise" of the wise, who, knowing how to make the dew of heaven fall upon it, make it produce fruits of an inestimable price.

*Cosmopolite* <note>A pseudonym for the alchemist Michael Sendivogius</note> very well expresses the advantages of this blessed earth: *He who knows how to congeal water in heat, and to join a spirit with it, shall truly find a thing a thousand times more precious than gold, and everything else.* Nothing comes near the worth of this earth and of this spirit, perfectly bound together according to the rules of our Art; they are the true *Mercury* and the true *Sulphur* of the *Philosophers*, the living male and the living female, who contain the seed which alone can beget a son more illustrious than his parents. ### The Fifth Key

The fifth key of our work is the fermentation of the Stone with the "perfect body" <note>In alchemy, the "perfect body" usually refers to gold or silver.</note> to create the Medicine of the Third Order. I will say nothing in particular regarding the operation of the third work, except that the perfect body is a necessary leaven <note>term: leaven; a substance, like yeast, used to make dough rise and transform its state.</note> for our paste. The spirit must create the union between the paste and the leaven, in the same manner that water moistens the meal and dissolves the leaven to compose a fermented paste fit to make bread. This comparison is very proper; Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy.</note> first made it, saying:

> "For as a paste cannot be fermented without a ferment, so when you shall have sublimed, cleansed, and separated the foulness from the dregs <note>original: "Faces" (faeces); the solid remains or impurities left after distillation or filtration.</note>; when you would join them, put a ferment in them, and make the water earth, so that the paste may be made a ferment."

On the subject of fermentation, the Philosopher <note>Likely referring to a specific alchemical authority or the collective wisdom of the "Sons of Art."</note> repeats the whole work here and shows that just as the entire lump of paste becomes all leaven by the action of the ferment which has been added to it, so all

<summary>This page describes the "Fifth Key" of alchemy, which is the fermentation of the Stone using a "perfect body" as a leaven, comparing the chemical transformation to the rising of bread dough.</summary>

<header>The HERMETICAL</header>

Through this operation, the entire philosophical mixture <note>original: "confection"</note> becomes a leaven suitable for fermenting new matter and multiplying it even unto infinity.

If you observe closely how bread is made, you will find the proportions you ought to maintain among the materials that compose our philosophical paste. Do the bakers not use more flour than leaven, and more water than both the leaven and the flour? The laws of nature are the rules you must follow in the practice of our entire <term>Magistery</term> <note>The "Mastery" or the complete process of creating the Philosopher's Stone</note>. I have given you all the necessary instructions on all the principal points, so it would be superfluous to tell you more—particularly concerning the final operations, about which the <term>Philosophers</term> <note>Alchemical authors and practitioners</note> have been less secretive than they were about the first operations, which are the foundations of the art.

### The Sixth Key

The sixth key teaches the multiplication of the <term>Stone</term>, achieved by repeating the same operation. This consists simply of opening and shutting, dissolving and coagulating, soaking <note>original: "imbibing"</note> and drying. By these means, the powers <note>original: "vertues"</note> of the <term>Stone</term> are infinitely increased.

<summary>The author explains that the alchemical "Stone" acts like yeast in bread-making to transform matter and introduces the Sixth Key, which focuses on increasing the Stone's power through repeated cycles of dissolution and coagulation.</summary>

# THE TRIUMPH

mented. <note>The text continues from the previous page's discussion on how the medicine is augmented.</note> As my design has not been to entirely describe the practice of the three medicines, but only to instruct you in the most important operations concerning the preparation of *mercury*—which the *philosophers* commonly pass over in silence to hide these mysteries from the profane, as they are only for the wise—I will tarry no longer upon this point. I will tell you nothing more of what relates to the projection <note>original: "Projection"; the process of casting the finished Stone onto a base metal to transmute it into gold or silver</note> of the medicine, because the success you expect does not depend on that final step alone.

I have given you very full instructions only on the third key, because it contains a long train of operations. Although these are simple and natural, they require a great understanding of the laws of nature and of the qualities of our matter, as well as a perfect knowledge of chemistry and of the different degrees of heat which are fitting for these operations.

I have conducted you by the straight way without any winding; and if you have well minded the road which I have pointed out to you, I am sure that you will go straight to the end without straying. I myself have undergone this painful journey for want of assistance, such as that which I give you in this letter, which comes from a sincere heart and a tender affection for all the true sons of the science <note>original: "Sons of the Science"; a term for initiated alchemists</note>. I would greatly pity you if, like me, after having discovered the true matter, you should spend fifteen years entirely in work, study, and meditation without being able to extract from the stone <note>original: "Stone"; the raw material of the Great Work</note> the precious juice which it encloses in its bosom. 

This failure happens for want of knowing the secret fire of the wise <note>original: "secret Fire of the Wise"; a specialized heat or chemical catalyst used in alchemy</note>, which causes a water that does not wet the hands to run out of this plant (which appears dry and withered). By a magical union with the "dry water" of the sea of the wise, this substance is dissolved into a viscous water—a mercurial liquor—which is the beginning, the foundation, and the key of our art. Convert, separate, and purify the elements as I have taught you, and you will possess the true mercury of the philosophers <note>original: "Mercury of the Philosophers"; not common quicksilver, but a purified alchemical principle</note>. This will give you the fixed sulphur <note>original: "fixt Sulphur"; the stable, active principle in alchemy</note> and the universal medicine.

But I warn you: even after you have arrived at the knowledge of the secret fire of the wise, you still shall not reach your goal on your first attempt.

# THE TRIUMPH

I have wandered for many years on the path that remains to be traveled to reach the mysterious fountain where the King bathes himself, is made young again, and takes on a new life free from all kinds of infirmities. <note>In alchemical symbolism, the "King" often represents gold or the perfected solar principle, and the "Fountain" or "Bath" refers to the mercurial solvent used to dissolve and renew it.</note> Besides this, you must know how to purify, heat, and animate this Royal Bath. It is to lend you a hand in this secret method that I have written at length on the third Key, where all these operations are described.

I wish with all my heart that the instructions I have given you may lead you directly to the goal. But remember, you sons of the science, <note>original: "Sons of the Science"; a traditional term for students of alchemy.</note> that the knowledge of our Magistery <note>The "Magistery" refers to the mastery of the Alchemical Great Work or the production of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> comes more through the inspiration of Heaven than from the insights we can acquire by ourselves. This truth is acknowledged by all philosophers; for that reason, it is not enough merely to work. Pray daily, read good books, and meditate night and day on the operations of Nature, and on what she may be able to do when she is assisted by the help of our Art. By these means, you will succeed without doubt in your undertaking.

This is all I had to say to you in this letter. I was not willing to make such a long discourse as the matter seemed to de— <note>The text cuts off mid-word; the catchword "mand" indicates the next page begins with "demand."</note>

# The HERMETICAL

command; neither have I told you anything but what is essential to our Art <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy, specifically the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>; so that if you know our Stone, which is the only Matter of our Stone, and if you have the understanding of our Fire—which is both Secret and Natural—you have the keys of the Art, and you can calcine <note>term: calcine; to reduce a substance to a powder through heat, though here used in a specialized alchemical sense.</note> our Stone. This is not done by the common calcination which is made by the violence of fire, but by a philosophical calcination which is purely natural.

Yet observe this with the most enlightened *Philosophers*: there is a difference between the common calcination made by the force of fire and the natural calcination. The first destroys the body and consumes the greatest part of its radical humidity <note>term: radical humidity; the essential, life-sustaining moisture believed to exist within all matter.</note>; but the second does not only preserve the humidity of the body while calcining it, but actually increases it considerably.

Experience will give you knowledge in the practice of this great truth, for you will indeed find that this philosophical calcination—which sublimes <note>term: sublimes; the process of turning a solid directly into a vapor and back to a solid to purify it.</note> and distills the Stone while calcining it—greatly augments its humidity. The reason is that the igneous spirit <note>term: igneous spirit; the "fiery" essence or spiritual energy contained within the natural fire.</note> of the natural fire is corporified <note>term: corporified; to be given a physical body or material form.</note> in the substances which are analogous to it.

# To the READER.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut square containing the letter 'I', topped with a small human face and surrounded by scrolling foliate patterns.</image-desc>

It has been thought proper (as mentioned in the Preface) to join with this treatise a translation of *THE ANCIENT WAR OF THE KNIGHTS*, <note>original: "The Ancient War of the Knights" refers to *Vom Rechten Wege*, a famous alchemical allegory often attributed to Limojon de Saint-Didier or earlier German sources.</note> translated directly from the original German. This was done so that the lovers of this science <note>In this context, "science" refers to Alchemy or the Hermetic arts.</note> might be able to compare it with the version translated from the French. 

Because that French version passed first into Latin, and from there into French, it has lost much of its original meaning, as will be discovered upon reading it. To make the work even more complete, some annotations have been added (from a German edition) regarding the most important points where the French translation differs from the German original.

### GOLD

The previously cited masters have written no untruth, and they all agree concerning my virtue <note>original: "virtue"; meaning inherent power or medicinal efficacy</note>. ### The STONE

I do not doubt it in any way, and it is very true that they indeed possessed the Art—excepting, however, some of those whom you have quoted. # The HERMETICAL

The masterpiece of Art <note>original: "Art" refers to Alchemy, the "Great Work" of transforming base metals into gold.</note> is begun with one thing and is ended with one thing. Although the Philosophers, in order to hide the Art, have pretended to point out many other ways—as, for instance: continual cooking (or digestion), mixing, sublimation, grinding <note>original: "Trituration"</note>, and drying <note>original: "Siccation"</note>, and as many other names (or allegories) as the process may be named with. <margin>37.</margin> However, the solution of the body is not brought about except in its own blood.

*Geber* <note>An influential 8th-century Arab alchemist, often cited as the father of chemistry.</note> says this: In the foundation (or in the root) of the nature of *Mercury* is sulfur, which perfects it (namely, the *Mercury*) by cooking and digesting it for a long time in the veins of the mines.

For no *philosopher* <note>In this context, "philosopher" refers to an alchemist or seeker of natural wisdom.</note> did ever express himself so plainly and openly.

it must have its natural time (or the time ordained by nature), like fruit growing on a tree.

# The HERMETICAL

land <note>original: *terra*</note>, which indeed sometimes may be understood as the Earth, and is interpreted this way in the Lord's Prayer: *As in heaven and on earth* <note>original: *Sicut in Coelo & in Terra*</note>, Thy will be done on Earth; but in this place it should be interpreted otherwise, namely, *in* the *Earth*.

---

By Number 9, the German original says: *To which* OUR *Stone answers*; but in the French version, the word *Our* is omitted; for it says, *the Stone answered*. It is supposed that the translator into Latin omitted the word *Our* first, perhaps not knowing that there is a difference between the "Stone of the Philosophers" and the substance called simply "the Stone." 

But the French commentator, being an ingenious philosopher, would not have omitted the word *Our*. Instead, he likely would have provided a learned explanation about it if he had found it in the Latin translation. For when the philosophers say *Our Stone*, they speak of it in that condition when it is being worked upon to be brought to perfection, and even beyond perfection <note>original: *plusquam perfection*</note>—namely, to the quality commonly called the *Philosophical Stone*. But when it is barely called *The Stone*, without any other title, in that case it may be applied to the Stone, either

<meta>catchword: generally,</meta>

<summary>This page analyzes the alchemical distinction between "the Stone" and "Our Stone," arguing that the latter refers to the substance as it is being refined toward perfection.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, The Ancient War of the Knights, Our Stone, Philosopher's Stone, translation errors, Latin translation, French translation</keywords>

--- Page 203 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of translation inaccuracies, focusing on how the Latin translator failed to distinguish between "The Stone" (the raw alchemical matter) and "Our Stone" (the matter once it is being refined by the alchemist), a distinction present in the original German text.</meta>

->TRIUMPH.<-

generally—namely, in each and every one of its states and conditions—or else it is used specifically (to distinguish the Stone of the *Philosophers*, or its primary matter) when it is still subject to the operations of Nature and has not yet reached the point where the artist <note>original: *Artist*—the alchemist who assists the natural process through their craft</note> may assist Nature through a careful imitation of her. But as soon as the *philosophers* begin working upon it, it is then properly and specifically called *Their Stone*.

It is true that the *philosophers* sometimes deliberately confuse these two names to hide the secrets from the unworthy <note>original: "unworthy"—those seeking alchemical knowledge for greed rather than spiritual or scientific wisdom</note>; but since the *German Philosopher* makes such a distinction, the translator into *Latin* should not have deprived the Hermetic <term>Hermetic: relating to the tradition of alchemy and secret wisdom attributed to Hermes Trismegistus</term> disciples of this same clarity. For they might have found the opportunity to reflect on why it is called *The Stone* at one time, and *Our Stone* at another. They could have also considered how the author speaks when he calls it merely *The Stone*, and what he intends when he distinguishes it with the word *Our*.

It was wrong, therefore, to be guilty of such an omission by failing to use the name *Our Stone* whenever it appeared in the *German* original, and instead always calling it merely *The Stone*.

<meta>This page continues the critique of translations, specifically looking at how the French version of the text misinterprets the grammatical nuances of the original German regarding the virtues of Gold and the Philosopher's Stone.</meta>

<header>The HERMETICAL</header>

-> * <-
-> * * <-

Regarding Number 10, the *German original* says: *Dear Gold, it is true, God has given you honor, beauty, and durability* <note>original: "Constancy"—in alchemy, this refers to a metal's "fixedness" or its ability to resist being destroyed by fire or acid</note>. This is translated by the *French* in this manner: *It is true, my dear gold, it is God who has given you THE honor, THE durability, and THE beauty.* 

This is said by the Stone <note>The Philosopher's Stone, often personified in alchemical dialogues</note> as an answer to the reproach that Gold put forward just before in Number 9. In that reproach, Gold uses the article *the*—specifically *the honor, etc.*—in order to claim for itself *all* the honor, beauty, and praise to be found in the operation and art of the *philosophers* <note>original: "Philosophers"—here referring to alchemists, the "philosophers by fire"</note>. 

The *German language* has this property: adding the article *The* encompasses everything, or the whole of a thing. Therefore, in its answer (according to the *German original*), the Stone greatly limits those virtues which Gold boasts of by omitting the article. It says, *God has given it honor*, which is to say, *God has given it some honor*. By this, it intends to suggest that Gold possesses only a part of such virtues. The Stone also gives us to understand in the following discourses that the Stone itself communicated those virtues to Gold while in the earth, and that

<summary>The author explains how linguistic differences between German and French affect the meaning of a dialogue between Gold and the Philosopher's Stone, specifically regarding whether Gold possesses all or only some of its celebrated virtues.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the linguistic nuances between German, Latin, and French translations of alchemical texts. It specifically addresses how the lack of articles in Latin led to a mistranslation in the French version of "The War of the Knights," affecting the perceived relationship between Gold and the Philosopher's Stone.</meta>

<page-num>25</page-num>
<header>TRIUMPH.</header>

Gold possesses honor, beauty, etc., and is indebted to the Stone for them. Therefore, Gold should not pretend to be the principal part—or even any part at all—of the Alchemical Work <note>original: "Philosophical Work"—the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone</note>, especially while excluding the Stone, which Gold wrongly insults.

However, it is not the fault of our eminent *French* philosopher <note>Likely referring to Alexandre-Toussaint de Limojon de Saint-Didier, who published "The Hermetic Triumph"</note> that the definite article [*The*] was used in the Stone's reply; this happened by accident. He did not translate the treatise called *The War of the Knights* from the *German* language, but from *Latin*, into which it had been translated first. Since the *Latin* language has no articles, it was impossible for him to know whether or where the article was used in the original German. Thus, it seems to have crept in by mere chance.

Nevertheless, it is true that the *Latin* translator is to blame. Had he been careful to render the author’s exact intention, he could have done so, even though it was impossible to use an article. He might have used these or similar words:

> It is true that God [has given] you SOME <note>original: *ALIQUID*—the author emphasizes that Gold only has a portion of these virtues, not the totality of them</note> honor, beauty, and constancy in fire <note>original: *Verum est, Deum tibi honoris, pulchritudinis & in igne constantiæ ALIQUID*</note>

<summary>The author explains that the French translation's error regarding Gold's virtues stems from the Latin translation's failure to capture the limiting sense of the original German. Because Latin lacks articles, the translator should have used specific words like "some" to indicate that Gold's qualities are partial and derived from the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>

<page-num>26</page-num>

[or *granted you your necessary measure* <note>original: "NECESSARIUM TIBI DIMENSUM concessisse"</note>].

->\*<-
->\* \*<-

Regarding the last mentioned number <note>Referring to the numbered sections or points in the treatise "The War of the Knights"</note>, there follows soon after: *But as for your disparaging me thus, you do it with untruths*, which words were left out in the French translation. Regardless of whose fault it is, it is certain that these words are very expressive (as is almost all the rest of the said treatise) and help us understand that the addition of common gold <note>original: "vulgar gold"—regular, physical gold as opposed to the specialized "philosophical" gold used by alchemists</note> to the Great Work <note>original: "philosophical Work"—the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone</note> tends to lead to the destruction of that work.

->\*<-
->\* \*<-

In point number 13, the German original says: *It being apparent that all of them achieve nothing, but use their gold in vain, and DESTROY THEMSELVES BY IT.* But the French says: *For it is unprofitably that they use their gold, and they ENDEAVOR TO DESTROY IT.*

The Latin translator has, in this passage, completely misunderstood the German (specifically regarding the word "destroy"). He likely wrote it this way: *They spend gold uselessly and destroy it* <note>original: "Aurum inutiliter impendunt & corrumpunt"</note>—which is entirely against the sense and meaning of the original.

<summary>The author identifies critical translation errors in French and Latin versions of an alchemical text, arguing that the original German warns that practitioners "destroy themselves" by using common gold incorrectly, rather than simply destroying the metal itself.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of translations of an alchemical treatise, focusing on the linguistic nuances between the German original and its French and Latin counterparts regarding the nature of gold and the role of Mercury.</meta>

<page-num>27</page-num>

<header>TRIUMPH.</header>

<sig>[*** 4]</sig>

The French philosopher <note>The "French philosopher" likely refers to the translator or commentator of the French edition of "The War of the Knights," possibly Alexandre-Toussaint Limojon de Saint-Didier.</note> clearly perceived that this passage could not be correct, and therefore did not translate it into French with the same meaning—namely, *that they destroy the gold*. Instead, he says, *that they endeavor to destroy it*, knowing very well that it is not possible for them to destroy gold.

The translator into Latin, finding the word "destroy" in the German, would have preserved the true meaning of the author if he had said: *We observe that they achieve nothing by these means, but rather spend their gold uselessly, and by that very act, failing miserably, they perish and fall into the greatest poverty.* <note>original Latin: "Animadvertimus, quod hisce nihil efficiant, sed potius aurum eorum inutiliter impendant, eoque ipso deficientes miserè pessum eant, & in summam egestatem incidant."</note>

Regarding the last cited number <note>Referring to the numbered sections or points in the alchemical treatise being analyzed.</note>, the German original says in the reply of Gold: *I will prove by the sayings of the philosophers that the Art <note>The "Art" refers to the Alchemical Art or the Great Work of transformation.</note> may be completed by me and my brother Mercury.* Regarding this, it should be noted that in the French version, the words *and my brother Mercury* have been omitted.

In point number 15, the German original says: *And they write without any winding expressions.* <note>Meaning they write straightforwardly, without convoluted metaphors or "circumlocution."</note> But the French says, *without any reserve.*

<meta>This page continues the critique of translations of the alchemical treatise "The Ancient War of the Knights." The author focuses on a specific discrepancy in section 19 regarding the characterization of "Mercury" and how translation errors led to unnecessary philosophical confusion.</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>

<header>The HERMETICAL</header>

<meta>catchword: "quidem" (indeed)</meta>

->*<-
->* *<-

In section number 19, the German original states: *“Your brother Mercury is indeed imperfect, yet for that reason he is not the Mercury of the Philosophers.”*—But the French version says: *“Your brother Mercury is indeed imperfect, and consequently, he is not the Mercury of the Wise.”*—If this were the case, it would indeed be true that the author of *The Ancient War of the Knights* <note>original: "The Ancient War of the Knights," a famous 17th-century alchemical work often attributed to Limojon de Saint-Didier or a German predecessor.</note> had contradicted himself. The *French* commentator is forced to admit this, and therefore has the character *Pyrophilus* <note>Pyrophilus is a name used in alchemical dialogues to represent a student or "lover of fire/wisdom."</note> ask a question about how to resolve this doubt. In response, *Eudoxus* <note>Eudoxus, meaning "of good repute," typically represents the master or experienced philosopher in these dialogues.</note> has done his best to uphold the honor and credibility of the author. However, there would have been no need to raise such a doubt, nor to resolve it, if the translation had been accurate and faithful to the original author's intent.

Nevertheless, as the proverb says, there is no situation so bad that some good cannot come of it. This was the case with this passage; it provided the commenting philosopher an opportunity to offer several important and useful insights regarding the text, even though he found it very difficult himself. Perhaps the translator into *Latin* was a *German* who rendered it as follows: *Your brother Mercury is...* <note>original Latin: "Frater tuus Mercurius est..."</note>

<summary>The author points out a significant translation error in "The Ancient War of the Knights" regarding the nature of Mercury, noting that the French translation created a logical contradiction that required complex explanations by later commentators.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, Mercury, The Ancient War of the Knights, Pyrophilus, Eudoxus, translation errors, textual criticism, German original vs French translation</keywords>

--- Page 209 ---
<meta>This page continues the linguistic analysis of a translation error in section 19 of "The Ancient War of the Knights." The author explains how a specific Latin phrase was misinterpreted by a French commentator due to differences in how German and French scholars traditionally used Latin grammar.</meta>

<page-num>29</page-num>

<header>The HERMETICAL TRIUMPH</header>

"...is indeed imperfect, but for that reason he is not the Mercury of the Sages—" <note>original: *quidem imperfectus, sed propterea non est Mercurius sapientum---.*</note> If it were written this way, it would be a *Germanism* <note>original: "Germanism," a linguistic term for a word or phrase borrowed from German or patterned after German grammar.</note>. Its meaning would be understood correctly by a *German* who understands *Latin*, but not easily by a *Frenchman* who is unfamiliar with the *Germanisms* that often occur among the *Germans* writing in the *Latin language*. 

One can see that the *French philosopher* understood the meaning of the *Latin words* this way: *He is imperfect, but for that very reason* (BECAUSE HE IS IMPERFECT) *he is not the Mercury of the Wise.* 

A *German*, however, understands it like this: *He is imperfect, but therefore* (ALTHOUGH HE IS IMPERFECT) *he is still not the Mercury of the Sages.* Or: *it does not follow as a consequence that he should be the Mercury of the Philosophers.* It is no argument at all that an imperfect *mercury* (of which there are many kinds) should, simply because of its imperfection, be the *Philosophical Mercury*. 

And thus, it is very likely that this was the fault of the translator. If he were determined to use the word *therefore* <note>original: *propterea*</note> (which might have been better omitted), he should at least have placed the word *not* <note>original: *non*</note> before it. For example: *He is not, however, for that reason the Mercury of the Sages* <note>original: *Non tamen propterea est Mercurius sapientum;*</note>; or, *It does not, however, follow from that...* <note>original: *Non tamen inde conficitur...*</note>

<meta>catchword: ...follow [part of the Latin "conficitur"]</meta>

<summary>The author explains how a mistranslation of a Latin phrase led a French commentator to misunderstand a German alchemical text, specifically concerning the nature of "Philosophical Mercury." This highlights the difficulty of translating technical nuances between different European languages and Latin.</summary>

<keywords>translation errors, Germanism, Philosophical Mercury, Mercury of the Sages, Latin grammar, Hermetic philosophy, linguistic nuance</keywords>

--- Page 210 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of a translation error regarding a "Germanism" in a Latin text. The author argues that a French commentator was misled by an ambiguous phrase, leading to a perceived contradiction in the alchemical theory being discussed.</meta>

<page-num>30</page-num>

<header>The HERMETICAL TRIUMPH</header>

*...that he is therefore the Mercury of the Sages.* <note>original: "...tur, eum propterea esse Mercurium sapientum." This completes the sentence from the previous page discussing whether an imperfect substance can be considered the true philosophical mercury.</note>

So this eminent philosopher (namely, the commentator) may have been led into an error by the ambiguity of this *Germanism* <note>original: "Germanism," referring to a Latin phrase constructed using German grammatical logic.</note>. One sees how he is forced to twist and turn in the answer given by *Eudoxus* <note>Eudoxus is a character in this philosophical dialogue who represents the knowledgeable teacher.</note> to shield the *German philosopher* from being blamed for the supposed contradiction. Whether he has succeeded in this, and whether the doubt raised by *Pyrophilus* <note>Pyrophilus is the character representing the student or seeker of knowledge.</note> has been sufficiently cleared and resolved by *Eudoxus's* answer (no matter how learned and useful it may be), is left to the judgment of those who are well-skilled in this Art <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy or Hermetic philosophy.</note>. 

Perhaps this passage appeared to the commentator as one of those which the philosophers use when they contradict themselves—either to hide secrets from the unworthy or for other reasons. This ought to be no obstacle to the true disciples of *Hermes* <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom the foundation of alchemy is attributed.</note>, who should use it as an opportunity to meditate even more on the reason and cause of such a contradiction; by doing so, they may unexpectedly discover some truths. For the *Cosmopolite* <note>Cosmopolite was the pseudonym of Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a famous alchemist.</note> says in the epilogue of his treatise: 

"Do not be disturbed that occasionally contradictions occur in my treatises, following the usual custom of the philosophers. Note well: You have need of them, if you understand them. It is not found..." <note>original: "Noli moveri, quod aliquando contradictoria in meis Tractatibus, more Philosophorum usitato, tibi occurrunt. N. B. Opus habes illis, si intelligis. Non reperitur"</note>

<meta>catchword: rosa</meta>

<summary>The author suggests that a French commentator's confusion over a Latin "Germanism" led to an unnecessary defense of a perceived contradiction. He concludes by noting that such contradictions are often intentional in alchemical texts to challenge and enlighten the true seeker.</summary>

"...a rose without thorns <note>original Latin: *rosa sine spinis*</note>." And further in his treatise on *Sulphur*, in the chapter concerning the three principles, he says: "Do not let it trouble you that writings appear contradictory to themselves in some places; for it is necessary that this be so, so that the art is not betrayed. Instead, choose those things which agree with Nature; take the roses, leave the thorns <note>original Latin: *Nec te moveat, quod scripta contraria sibi videantur in aliquibus locis; ita enim opus est, ne ars proderetur: Tu autem elige ea, quae cum Natura conveniunt; accipe rosas, relinque spinas.*</note>." In the same manner, our eminent French commentator has (probably) taken this reply regarding the Stone to be one of these intentional contradictions, which he designed to reconcile through the answer given by *Eudoxus* <note>Eudoxus is the teacher figure in this dialogue.</note>.

Regarding number 32, the German original says: "But as for me, I have an imperfect body; a pure, penetrating, <term>tinging</term><note>To "tinge" in alchemy means to transform or dye a substance, usually referring to the power of the Philosopher's Stone to turn base metals into gold.</note>, and constant spirit." The French version gives these words with some variation, namely: "I have an imperfect body, a steadfast soul, a penetrating <term>tincture</term>." This, nevertheless, is much to the same purpose. Only this ought to be added: there are two editions extant in the German language which differ from the rest and ascribe a *perfect* body to the Stone. However, according to the first edition printed in 1604, and several others, the Stone *has an imperfect body*.

At this point, it should be noted that both "perfect" and "imperfect" are correct, considering that the Stone is in a different state at different times. This is sufficiently clarified by the answer that *Eudoxus* <note>Eudoxus is the teacher figure in this philosophical dialogue.</note> gives to the objection made by *Pyrophilus* <note>Pyrophilus, whose name means "lover of fire," is the student figure.</note> regarding the aforementioned number.

If, however, one were to follow the two previously mentioned editions, there would have been no need for the objection of *Pyrophilus* or the answer of *Eudoxus*.

---

The passage at number 34 is the one mentioned by the *French commentator* in his preface, where the *ancient philosophers* are quoted as saying: *Our Mercury can only be obtained from soft, unadulterated bodies.*

The reason why this passage was corrupted when it was translated into *Latin* seems to stem from the *German* word ***ÆCHT*** <note>Modern German: *echt*. The author is explaining a philological error where a German technical term was mistranslated into Latin.</note>, which means "true," "proper," "legitimate," "uncorrupted," "standard," and "fine." It generally serves to distinguish a genuine matter from a counterfeit. However, because this same word has some similarity to the—

<summary>This page explains that the Philosopher's Stone can be described as both perfect and imperfect depending on its stage of development. It also begins to explain a translation error from German to Latin involving the word "ÆCHT" (genuine) regarding the source of alchemical Mercury.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological discussion of how a German-to-Latin translation error occurred. The previous page explained that the word "ÆCHT" (meaning "true" or "genuine") was misinterpreted due to its similarity to another word.</meta>

<page-num>33</page-num>
<header>TRIUMPH.</header>

Because of the word *ACHT* <note>original: "ACHT". In German, *acht* means "eight," while *echt* (or the archaic *æcht*) means "genuine." This confusion led a translator to write "eight" where they should have written "true."</note>, which signifies "eight," the translator was led into the mistake that the commentator criticizes.

Whether the *French* commentator corrected this gross error using the original text, or whether he thought it proper to remove those obscure and improper words and insert others in their place, is not known. However, it is certain that if the latter is the case, a *philosopher* like himself has the authority to do it—especially when he indicates his intention to do so, as this commentator has done. Furthermore, as a master, he has the prerogative to teach the disciples of *Hermes* <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary figure to whom the foundation of alchemy (Hermeticism) is attributed.</note> using whatever words he thinks appropriate to show where the *Mercury of the Wise* <term>Mercury of the Wise: the essential "spirit" or "living" solvent that alchemists sought to extract from base metals</term> is to be obtained.

It is also noteworthy that this same commentator, by saying: "*It is not possible to attain the possession of our Mercury in any other way than by means of two bodies, of which one cannot receive its required perfection without the other,*" has arranged this passage in such a manner that it agrees with the intent of the original, even though the words are different.

In section number 35, the *French* translation says: *The (Universal) Medicine is therefore made out of one thing, which is the water and the spirit of the body.* But the *German* original says: *Therefore, a medicine is made out of one thing, which is the water of the body and spirit.* This latter version, however, in no way contradicts the sense and meaning of our commentator, who says himself in his third Key: *Within the Wine of the Philosophers (which appears in the form of water) there are three different substances and three natural principles of all bodies—namely <note>original: "viz." (videlicet)</note> Salt, Sulphur, and Mercury, which are the spirit, the soul, and the body.* <term>Salt, Sulphur, and Mercury: Known as the "Tria Prima," these were the three fundamental ingredients that alchemists believed composed all material things.</term>

And thus it may be said, according to the sense of the original: *That thing out of which the medicine is made is the water (namely, the Wine of the Wise <term>Wine of the Wise: a term for the "mercurial water" or solvent used to dissolve metals in the first stage of the Great Work</term>), of the body (namely, the body in the water, which is the male), and of the spirit (namely, the body and spirit; for the author himself considers them all together in the previously cited passage as the feminine).* And with this, compare what is said in section number 37.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of "Lunar Vulcan" from the previous page, specifically referencing commentary number 4. It explores the dual nature of alchemical fire—internal and external—citing various classical and hermetic authorities to define the role of fire in the transformation of "the Matter."</meta>

<page-num>36</page-num>
<header>The HERMETICAL</header>

(mentioned in Number 4) is meant the secret and natural Fire of the *Philosophers*.

It is true that this Fire is, in itself, only one Fire; but regarding its location, it is called both Interior and Exterior. For instance, the aforementioned *Eudoxus* <note>Eudoxus: A character in this dialogue representing the knowledgeable alchemist.</note> calls it *the natural and interior Fire* because, as he says, *it is brought into action by the exterior fire*.

*Egyptian Philosophy* <note>original: Philosophia Ægyptiaca</note> on the other hand says: *Just as Osiris and Isis are spouses, so they are also brother and sister (according to the testimony of Plutarch), existing under a certain mystical relationship. To them is added a third, Typhon (perhaps the internal fire of the matter), a black and burning Spirit, who dissects his uterine brother Osiris into very tiny pieces. To these, they add* VULCAN, OR THE EXTERNAL FIRE. <note>original: "Osiris & Isis uti conjuges sunt, sic & frater & soror, Plutarcho teste, sub mystica quadam ratione habentes, quibus additur tertius quidam, Typhon (forte ignis internus materiæ) niger & ardens Spiritus, qui Osiridem fratrem uterinum in minutissima membra dissecat. Hisce adnumerant VULCANUM SEU IGNEM EXTERNUM."</note>

Therefore, if understood according to this latter sense—namely, that it is Exterior—it is not part of the Matter, as *Artephius* <note>Artephius: A famous 12th-century alchemist to whom several influential treatises were attributed.</note> says; but if understood in the first sense, it is part of the Matter, according to the meaning of *Empedocles* <note>Empedocles: A Greek pre-Socratic philosopher who proposed the four-element theory.</note>.

Regarding *Vulcan* being called *Lunar*, the author of *The War of the Knights* agrees with the great *Hermes* <note>Hermes Trismegistus: The legendary Hellenistic figure to whom the Hermetic corpus and the Emerald Tablet are attributed.</note>, who says: *Its father is the Sun, and its mother is the Moon* <note>original: "Pater ejus Sol, mater verò Luna"</note>; and by this the quality of the philosophical *Vulcan* is suggested—specifically, that its heat must be tempered

<summary>This page defines the "secret and natural fire" of alchemy as having both an interior and exterior nature, using the Egyptian myth of Osiris, Isis, and Typhon as an allegory. It concludes by linking the "Lunar Vulcan" to the cooling, tempered heat associated with the Moon in Hermetic tradition.</summary>

<keywords>alchemical fire, Eudoxus, Osiris, Isis, Typhon, Vulcan, Artephius, Empedocles, Hermes Trismegistus, Lunar Vulcan, interior fire, exterior fire</keywords>

--- Page 217 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "Lunar Vulcan" (the secret fire), explaining how the heat of the "Sun" (the internal fire) is balanced by the moisture of the "Moon" to create a tempered alchemical process. It cites various authorities like Algahel, Hermes, and the Cosmopolite.</meta>

<page-num>37</page-num>
<header>TRIUMPH.</header>

[tem]pered by the humidity <note>In alchemy, humidity or moisture represents the passive, cooling principle, often associated with the Moon or Mercury.</note> proceeding from *Luna* <note>original: Luna; the Moon</note>; for *Algahel*, in his *Advice on the Conjunction of the Matter of the Sun and Moon* <note>original: Consil. Conj. de massa Solis & Lunæ</note>, says: "The Sun <note>original: Sol</note> (which our author calls *Vulcan*) is the source of all heat; the Moon, however, is the mistress of humidity." And *Hermes* <note>Hermes Trismegistus: The legendary founder of alchemy.</note> says: "Humidity comes from the dominion of the Moon; richness <note>original: pinguedo; literally 'fatness' or 'oiliness,' referring to the combustible or 'sulfurous' principle</note> comes from the dominion of the Sun." 

For that reason, the *Cosmopolite* <note>The Cosmopolite: A pseudonym for the famous alchemist Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636).</note> says: "Internal heat is much more effective than this elemental fire, but it is tempered by earthly water, which day by day penetrates the pores of the earth and cools it. In the same way, the air that circles the globe day by day tempers the celestial sun and its heat." <note>original: Calor intrinfecus multo efficacior est hoc igne elementali, sed temperatur terrenâ aquâ, quæ de die in diem poros terræ penetrat illamque refrigerat: Sic solem cœlestem & ejus calorem temperat aër, qui de die in die circumvolat orbem.</note> 

And at another place, he says: "Just as the central Sun has its own sea and perceptible raw water, so the celestial Sun also has its own sea and subtle, imperceptible water." <note>original: Sicuti Sol centralis habet suum mare & aquam crudam perceptibilem; sic Sol cœlestis habet etiam suum mare & aquam subtilem & inperceptibilem.</note> 

And thus, for the reasons mentioned above, the *Vulcan* <note>Vulcan: The Roman god of fire; here used to represent the alchemical fire used in the transformation of matter.</note> of the philosophers must be "Lunar."

As for the French translation of these words, they are quite different; for that translation calls it "Lunatic Vulcan." It is not to be supposed that these words are given with any meaning other than what was mentioned before. After all, "lunatic" can mean "insane" in another sense, and it cannot be supposed that the title of *The War of the Knights* would imply that the Philosopher's Stone...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on controlling alchemical heat, warning against an excessive "mad Vulcan" (violent fire) and emphasizing the balanced union between the "Sun" and "Moon" (the active and passive principles).</meta>

<page-num>38</page-num>
<header>The HERMETICAL</header>

[phi]losophers is to be prepared with the assistance of such a mad *Vulcan* <note>In alchemy, 'Vulcan' refers to the fire or the heat used in the process. A 'mad' Vulcan is an uncontrolled or excessive fire that ruins the chemical work.</note>. On the contrary, the *Turba* <note>The *Turba Philosophorum* (Assembly of the Philosophers) is one of the oldest and most influential alchemical texts, written as a dialogue between different thinkers.</note> says: "Do not heat the bath of Citrinus <note>Citrinus refers to the yellowing or 'citrination' stage of the alchemical process, personified here as a character.</note> and his wife too much, lest they be deprived of sense and motion." <note>original: *Ne incendatis balneum Citrini & ejus uxoris plurimum, ne sensu & motu priventur.*</note> Likewise: "Honor the King and his wife, and do not burn them with too much fire." <note>original: *Item, Veneramini Regem & suam uxorem & nolite eos comburere nimio igne.*</note> And therefore the fountain (which *Bernhard Trevisan* <note>Bernhard Trevisan (1406–1490) was an Italian alchemist known for his allegorical stories about the 'King' and the 'Fountain.'</note> speaks of) in which the King bathes himself is secured by a threefold fence. In the exposition of the letter *From the Sun to the Moon* <note>original: *Solis ad Lunam*</note>, are these words:

> "I, the Moon, increasing in solar light, am cold and moist, because I am the mother of humors; and you, the Sun, are hot and dry, which is the principle of the generation of all things, when we are coupled—that is, joined—together." <note>original: *Ego Luna crescens in lumine Solari frigida & humida, quia sum mater humorum & tu Sol, calidus & siccus, quod est principium generationis omnium, quando copulati fuerimus, i. e. conjuncti.*</note>

*Note Well:* In equality of state, that is, in proportion and permanence <note>original: *In Æqualitate statûs, i. e. in proportione & mansione.*</note>. Now such a coupling as that cannot be brought about by a mad *Vulcan*, and therefore the book *The War of the Knights* <note>A famous alchemical dialogue, often published alongside the 'Hermetical Triumph,' detailing the struggle between different chemical principles.</note> could not suppose that the followers of Nature had made use of such violence to make the Stone of the Philosophers.

The reason why the *French* translation says *Lunatic Vulcan* probably comes from the fact that the translators into *Latin* made another error in this passage,

It also addresses potential translation errors regarding the nature of the "secret fire."</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the discussion from page 38 regarding the proper control of alchemical fire (Vulcan). It specifically addresses a linguistic error in a previous commentary that confused "lunatic" (unstable) fire with "lunar" (tempered) fire.</meta>

<page-num>39</page-num>

# T R I U M P H.

...and said: "By the help of a lunatic Vulcan" <note>original: *Auxilio Vulcani Lunatici*. In alchemy, "Vulcan" represents fire; a "lunatic" fire is one that is unstable or uncontrolled.</note>; when he should have said, "By the help of a lunar Vulcan" <note>original: *Auxilio Vulcani Lunaris*. A "lunar" fire refers to a gentle, steady heat associated with the moon or the silver stage of the work.</note>. 

This error, however, is not to be attributed to our eminent commentator <term>A scholar who provides explanatory notes on a primary text.</term>, who was compelled to receive the words exactly as they were given to him. It is only in order to clear our learned commentator from the suspicion of making such an error that this note <note>original: *Annotation*</note> has been made regarding it.
